<margin>Colossians 2:18.</margin>
It is true that St. Paul says, *Let no one disqualify you, insisting on self-abasement and worship of angels, taking his stand on visions he has not seen*. But I am going to prove that St. Paul does not at all condemn the practice of praying to Angels to pray to God for us, nor of asking them for the duties of their Angelic ministry; nor does he forbid honoring them as friends and servants of God. Rather, he condemns the heresy of Simon the Magician <note>Also known as Simon Magus, a figure from the New Testament (Acts 8) whom early Church writers identified as the first heretic.</note>, whom an Ancient writer calls the <margin>Irenaeus</margin> *Source and Father of all heretics*. This Simon, following the doctrine of the Platonists <note>Followers of the philosopher Plato, whose ideas about a hierarchy of spiritual beings influenced early religious thought.</note>, taught that the Angels were Gods by nature—albeit lesser Gods—and that they had made the world with God's permission and had spoken to the prophets. He taught that, in this capacity, one must give them Divine worship, and through them, as if by steps, go to the sovereign God and appease the invisible God, which, they said, could be done by no other means.

It is in this way that the excellent servant of God, St. John Chrysostom, understands this passage: *There are some*, he says <meta>The catchword "il" (he) links to the next page of the manuscript.</meta>

# Article XI.

AT the first point of this article, which establishes the difference between **mortal and venial sins** <note>In Catholic theology, "mortal" sins are grave offenses that break one's relationship with God, while "venial" sins are lesser offenses that do not destroy the state of grace.</note>, they follow their own fashion, abandoning the understanding that the Spirit of God has given to His Church to follow that of their **private spirit**, manifested to the world for a century and a half <note>A reference to the Protestant Reformation, which the author dates to roughly 150 years prior to this writing.</note>.

They will not say it is the first, Our Lord not having come to regulate the Civil judgment of the Tribunals of that people. <margin>Deut. 27:26</margin>
He who sins against the law is *cursed*, I admit: because his sin is mortal. But venial sin, such as an idle word or another of very slight consideration, is not against the law, but outside the law. It does not, therefore, deserve this curse.

<margin>Rom. 6:23</margin>

<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on temporal punishment and addresses Protestant critiques regarding the state of the soul after death. Specifically, it responds to the argument that if the Church prays for the dead to have "peace," they must not be in a state of suffering (Purgatory).</meta>

...faith of Jesus: And these are, as Theodore Beza <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605) was a prominent Protestant reformer and the successor to John Calvin in Geneva.</note> says, the Martyrs who with their patience have overcome the Devil, the world, sin, and death, and for whom the faithful witness of the Faith <gloss>a</gloss> of the golden ages <note>A reference to the era of the early Church Fathers, particularly St. Augustine.</note> does not wish for us to pray, but says that we must commend ourselves to their prayers, because they have nothing to suffer after this life: but rest in Heaven from the labors they endured in their martyrdom.

It is true that the Canon of the Messe <gloss>b</gloss> <note>The "Canon" is the most solemn part of the Catholic Mass, which has remained largely unchanged for centuries.</note> prays for those who sleep the sleep of peace: but one must not imagine, however, that those who inserted this prayer into this Canon did not believe in Purgatory, since there was nothing more universal in the Church's antiquity than this belief: but what they add—and what the [Protestant] Ministers intentionally remain silent about—clarifies this objection; *We pray Thee, Lord, to grant them, and all those who rest in Jesus Christ, the place of refreshment, of light, and of PEACE.* The Church asks for peace for those who are already in peace. She signifies, therefore, that they enjoy an imperfect and incipient peace, outside the troubles of the earth, the passions, temptations of sin, and the perils of Hell; She wishes for them a perfect and consummate peace in Heaven.

---

<gloss>a</gloss> *St. Augustine, Sermon 17 on the words of the Apostle, ch. 1. One does not pray for Martyrs at the Altar of God. It is an insult to pray for a Martyr to whose prayers we ought to be commended.* <note>original Latin: "Pro Martyribus non oratur ad Altare Dei..."</note>

<gloss>b</gloss> *Remember, O Lord, Thy servants and handmaids who have gone before us with the sign of Faith and sleep in the sleep of peace. To them, O Lord, and to all who rest in Christ, we beseech Thee to grant a place of refreshment, light, and PEACE.* <note>original Latin: "Memento Domine famulorum famularumq; tuarum..." This is the "Memento of the Dead" from the Roman Canon.</note>

<summary>The author argues that the Catholic Mass's prayer for the deceased to find "peace" does not disprove Purgatory, as it refers to a transition from earthly struggle to the perfect peace of Heaven. Heaven, which is called by excellence Jerusalem, which means "vision of peace."

It is a matter so clear that our Lord wished to exhort men to agree amicably among themselves—because He says so in the 5th chapter of St. Matthew <note>Matthew 5:25: "Settle matters quickly with your adversary who is taking you to court."</note>—that it is no wonder both the ancient and modern Doctors of the Church have recognized it. Since there is no pardon of sin except *in this Age and in the Age to come*, it is certain that the sin which is forgiven in neither one nor the other of these Ages is never eternally forgiven, as our Lord expresses in St. Mark:
<margin>Mark 3:29.</margin>

<margin>Bellarmine, *On Purgatory*, Book 1, Chapter 4.</margin>
Bellarmine says that what we infer from these words of the Lord does not follow according to the rules of the *Dialecticians* <note>Philosophers or logicians specializing in formal reasoning.</note>; but he says (which the Gentlemen Ministers conceal with their usual "good faith") that it follows according to the rule of *prudence* <note>Practical wisdom or sound judgment; Bellarmine argues that even if it is not a strict logical necessity, it is the most sensible interpretation.</note>: for otherwise, he says, *we would make our Lord speak very foolishly [if He said] this sin will not be forgiven in this Age, nor in the one to come.* If in the Age to come no sin were forgiven, it would be as foolishly said, he adds, as saying the King of Spain will not grant a pardon in the Court of Spain, nor in that of Brabant <note>Brabant was a duchy in the Low Countries under the rule of the Spanish Habsburgs; the analogy suggests that mentioning a second location for pardons implies pardons are actually possible there.</note>, if in that of Brabant he never granted pardons at all.

Now, not all fall into the eternal punishments—specifically those who, after death, suffer temporal ones: for to some, that which is not forgiven in this age is forgiven in the age to come (Matthew 12), that is to say, so that they are not punished with the torment of the age to come.

The *gold, silver, and precious stones* are *the work that will remain*, and for which the *Architect will receive a reward*. The *wood, hay, and stubble* are *the work that will burn*, and for which the *Architect will suffer loss*. St. Paul, therefore, does not designate the *faithful* by *gold, silver, and precious stones*; nor...

by the wood, hay, and stubble, [they do not mean] hypocrites—as your Doctors ridiculously claim—but the work that they build.

In the second place, they contradict their own Interpreters; you have only to look at the Bible of Diodati <note>Giovanni Diodati (1576–1649), a Protestant theologian whose Italian translation of the Bible was highly influential and often included extensive scholarly notes.</note>, and it will tell you that St. Paul designates *by the gold, silver, and precious stones: the pure, solid, Divine, and precious Doctrine of the Gospel;* and by the wood, hay, and stubble *the terms of human art, the ornaments of rhetoric, and philosophical questions which do not falsify the substance of the Gospel, but deform its face.*

Finally, they contradict all the ancient Doctors whom they themselves call Holy and orthodox, *who* by wood, hay, and stubble *understand a doctrine that is not heretical or evil, but curious, useless, and superfluous;* and by comparison to this doctrine, the sins that St. Augustine * calls *light, small, daily, and venial,* and which Jesus Christ had called *motes* <note>Referring to the "mote" or splinter in one's eye mentioned in Matthew 7:3.</note>. By this fleeting fire of which St. Paul said, *He shall be saved, yet so as by fire,* it is not the capital sins, but the SMALL ones that are PURGED: says St. Augustine.

Whether this interpretation is not more consistent with the text—*which distinguishes the builder from his work which burns just as hay, wood, and stubble burn in the fire*—than that of Oecumenius <note>A 10th-century Greek bishop whose commentaries were often attributed to a much earlier "Orthodox" era by early modern scholars.</note> who understands by this wood, hay, and stubble *the workers of iniquity,* taking the worker for his work, and the builder for what he builds, I leave the judgment to you: the text, reason, and authority

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of prayers for the dead, moving from Biblical proofs discussed on the previous page to the historical testimony of the Early Church Fathers from the 3rd to 5th centuries.</meta>

* St. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), Archbishop of Constantinople, renowned for his preaching and known as the "Golden-Mouthed."</note> recommends helping the deceased; not with tears, but with prayers, supplications, and alms, and never to grow weary of assisting them in this way; and he says that these things were not ordained recklessly by the **APOSTLES**.

* And St. Cyprian <note>Cyprian of Carthage (c. 210–258), an important early Christian bishop and martyr.</note>, who lived in the year 250 of Our Lord, relates the public practice of prayers for the dead to the ordinance of the Bishops who were his predecessors.

I could cause a cloud of witnesses <note>A reference to Hebrews 12:1, suggesting a vast number of historical authorities.</note> to fly over the heads of these Gentlemen <note>A common polemical term used by the author to refer to the Protestant reformers, likely the Calvinists.</note> who unanimously testify to the common use of these prayers throughout the entire universe, to make you admit that it is a very great temerity for a few newcomers to abolish what the **APOSTLES** ordained, and to mock what the entire Church for about 17 centuries has practiced throughout the world. But what has been said above is enough to make known the very insolent folly of all those who dispute whether one should do what the entire Church does throughout the Universe, *as the great St. Augustine says.

* St. Chrysostom, *Homily 41 on 1 Corinthians*: "Let the deceased be helped not by tears, but by prayers, supplications, and alms." <note>original: "Juuetur mortuus non lachrymis: sed precibus, supplicationibus, eleemosynis."</note> And further down: "Let us not grow weary of bringing help to the dead, offering prayers for them." <note>original: "ne fatigemur mortuis auxilium ferre preces pro illis offerentes."</note>

* St. Cyprian, *Book 1, Letter 9*: "The Bishops our predecessors established that if any brother, upon passing, should name a Clergyman as a guardian or trustee, no offering should be made for him, nor should the sacrifice be celebrated for his falling asleep." <note>original: "Epiſcopi Anteceſſores noſtri ſtatuerant ſi quis frater excedens ad tutelam vel curam Clericum nominaret, non offerretur pro eo, nec ſacrificium pro eius dormitione celebraretur." This disciplinary rule implies that offering "sacrifice" (the Mass) for the deceased was the standard practice.</note>

* St. Augustine, *Letter 118 to Januarius*: "If the entire Church throughout the world practices something, to dispute whether it should be done is a matter of the most insolent madness." <note>original: "Si quid tota per orbem frequentat Eccleſia quin ita faciendum ſit diſputare inſolentiſſimæ inſaniæ eſt."</note>

And indeed, what would they say to the most faithful testimony \* that he gives: that prayer for the Dead came from the *Tradition of the Fathers*, and was observed by the *whole Church*? And that he who delays his repentance until the next World shall be **PURGED by a FIRE of PURGATION which is not eternal**?

So that you might not see too clearly into this fire, and that by believing in it you might fear it, and by fearing it you might work toward your sanctification with fear and trembling; they throw dust in your eyes. Instead of answering these so formal words, they go—[against the rules of debate, which forbid claiming to prove the opposite of what the Adversary says until one has first destroyed his reasons]—to bring you another passage from this Doctor, \* which says that *there are two places, and there is no third*, and that *he who has not earned the right to reign with Christ will undoubtedly perish with the Devil*.

A single word brings down their claim by telling you that he is speaking of places of eternal dwelling, which are only two: namely, *Paradise* and *Hell*. This is clear from what these Gentlemen admit is in Sermon 232, against drunkenness; for St. Paul teaches us that there is no 3rd place

Leaving them there, I will move on to examine how they understand the words of the Prophet Zechariah. They say *it concerns the deliverance of the Jews from the Babylonian Captivity*. To see that this interpretation is not probable, one only needs to read the text. In the preceding verses, God predicts to Zion the coming of the King mounted on the foal of a donkey <note>A reference to Jesus’s triumphal entry into Jerusalem.</note>, saying that he would speak of peace to the nations, and his dominion would be from one sea to the other, and from the river to the ends of the earth. And then he adds: *As for you, by the blood of your covenant, I have set your prisoners free from the pit where there was no water* <note>original: "la fosse ou il n'y auoit point d'eau." This is said regarding the blood of Jesus Christ, as Diodati <note>Giovanni Diodati, a prominent Protestant theologian and Bible translator; the author is citing a Protestant authority to undermine the local Calvinists' argument.</note> admits. But what need was there for this blood for the deliverance from the Babylonian Captivity? Could that not have been achieved just as well without this blood, as was the deliverance from the Captivity in Egypt? You only have to look at the fabric of the text, which speaks continually of the King of Zion, of the extent of his dominion, and of the salvation acquired by his blood for the faithful.

<meta>This page continues the author's theological defense of the "Descent into Hell." While the previous page established early Church tradition (Cyprian, Jerome, Cyril), this page transitions into a linguistic and logical analysis of Scripture, specifically addressing Protestant interpretations of the words "soul" and "hell" in the Psalms and the Book of Acts.</meta>

I admit that the word **hell** <note>original: "enfer," here referring to the Hebrew *Sheol* or the Greek *Hades*, the realm of the dead.</note> sometimes signifies the **grave**, and that the word **soul** means at times the **life**, at times the **person**, and there was no need to pile up passage after passage to prove a thing that no one disputes. But as they also cannot deny that this word **soul** properly signifies that spiritual substance which gives man his being and distinguishes him from the beasts, they must also admit that the question is whether it is in this sense that the word is taken here, or in those they have outlined.

They say that David and St. Peter speak of the resurrection of Our Lord—I admit it. Therefore, he does not speak of the departure of his soul from hell—this I deny. On the contrary, I say that he affirms and presupposes it. But nothing is more inconsistent than what they say to show they do not know what to decide: that David and St. Peter mean by "soul" either the life, or the person, or the body; for if it is one, it is not the other, these being things really distinct and entirely different.

It is clear they do not mean the *life* of Jesus Christ, as this could not be "not left" in the grave if it were not there, there being only the dead body.

Nor his *Person*, for it also could not be "not left" in the grave, since it was not there; only the body was present, which was not his person, for the soul necessary for the personality of the **God-man** <term>God-man: a translation of the Greek "Theanthropos," referring to the union of divine and human natures in the person of Jesus Christ.</term> was missing.

Nor his *Body*, for he distinguishes it immediately afterward:
> *You will not leave my soul in **hell**, and will not allow your HOLY ONE to see **corruption**.* <note>A quotation of Psalm 16:10, cited by St. Peter in Acts 2:27 to argue that Christ’s body did not decay because of his immediate resurrection.</note>

We must therefore understand "soul" in the proper sense,

But I would certainly have been a hypocrite if I had persevered in preaching against these truths, as they and you wanted me to do.

# Article XIII.

These gentlemen are trying to make you believe that on the point of **free will** <note>The ability of humans to make choices unconstrained by certain factors, such as fate or divine intervention.</note>, I am a *Pelagian* and a *Semi-Pelagian* <note>Pelagianism was a 5th-century belief that humans could achieve salvation through their own effort without divine grace; Semi-Pelagianism suggested that humans could take the first step toward God on their own, but needed grace for the rest. Both were declared heretical by the Catholic Church.</note>. But if you look closely at what I say about it, it will be easy for you to see the truth of it, and the falsehood of what they attribute to me.

<margin>Session 6.</margin>
I say, along with Session 6 of the Council of Trent <note>A major 16th-century council of the Catholic Church that defined its doctrines in response to the Protestant Reformation.</note>, that man’s free will has been so wounded and weakened by sin that he cannot, by himself and without the help of **Divine Grace**, perform what is good; but nevertheless, he has not lost all liberty to such an extent that he does not still possess enough, with the help of this Grace, to do the good that God commands him.

The **Roman Church** produces an innumerable number of proofs of this truth through Holy Scripture and through the common consensus of the Church, which everyone holds to be beyond suspicion in the Faith. I have provided you with a sample of them; and instead of responding to them and then bringing forward their own, as the order of any regulated dispute requires, they first go about prejudicing you with other proofs before even touching those that I put forward.

They say that my Article "smells of" *Semi-Pelagianism*; to act as they ought, they were obliged to show you what this *Semi-Pelagianism* is, and then confront my thesis with it to demonstrate their conformity.

Not having the courage to do this, they immediately change their "battery" <note>A military metaphor for changing the position or focus of their cannons or line of attack.</note> and go to oppose me with passages that **Calvin** <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), a principal figure in the Protestant Reformation whose followers emphasized the total depravity of man and the absolute necessity of grace.</note> indicated they should use to oppose the truth currently in question.

I am sorry not to observe the necessary order here

<summary>In Article XIII, the author defends his position on free will against accusations of heresy, aligning himself with the Council of Trent's view that while human will is weakened by sin, it still cooperates with divine grace.</summary>

With these decrees my Article agrees, saying that free will is so wounded <note>original: "nauré," an archaic term meaning wounded or injured.</note> and weakened that it cannot by itself, and without the help of Divine Grace, perform what is good. <meta>catchword: Good</meta>

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of his views on free will, specifically refuting charges of heresy. He is distinguishing his position from Pelagianism by arguing that while humans possess a weakened free will, it is utterly dependent on Divine Grace to perform any spiritual good.</meta>

Far from this savoring of <term>Pelagianism: A 5th-century heresy which taught that humans could achieve salvation through their own willpower without divine help.</term> or <term>Semipelagianism: A doctrine suggesting that humans can take the first step toward God on their own, though grace is needed to complete the process.</term>, it is directly opposed to them. These ancient heresies claimed that by natural forces alone one could merit the Grace of God and salvation, and that Grace was only given to make doing good *more easy*, whereas without it one could still do good, but with greater difficulty. This is what we are taught by * St. Augustine, who wrote against Pelagius. *To this end (says he of what this heretic taught) the Grace of God is given to all so that what they are commanded to do by free will they may do MORE EASILY through Grace.*

Nevertheless, as God commands the good, He promises a reward if it is done, and threatens punishment if it is not. He forbids evil, and promises if it is avoided, and threatens if it is not. Something of our own is required; it is this freedom that remains to us toward the good—though so weak that without Grace it cannot perform the good that is commanded. But being forestalled <note>original: "prevenue," referring to 'prevenient' grace, the theological concept of grace that "comes before" or precedes human decision.</note>, aided, and accompanied by this grace, it freely consents to the duty presented to it, and toward which it is gently led. Thus, this Holy Father also says * *we are not made faithful except by our free will, and yet*

---

* <note>original: "S. Aug. Ep. 106. ad Bonif. c. Pel. Alio loco ait ideò Dei Gratiam omnibus dari ut quod facere per liberum iubentur arbitrium faciliùs possint implere per Gratiam."</note> St. Augustine, Letter 106 to Boniface against the Pelagians. In another place he says that the Grace of God is given to all so that what they are commanded to do through free will they might be able to fulfill more easily through Grace.

* <note>original: "S. Aug. Ep. 107. ad Vitalem c. Pel. Ita fit ut neque fideles fiant nisi libero arbitrio. Et tamen illius Gratia fideles fiant qui eorum potestate tenebrarum liberavit arbitrium. Sic & Dei Gratia non negatur, & liberum ita defenditur, ut humilitate solidetur, non elatione præcipitetur arbitrium."</note> St. Augustine, Letter 107 to Vitalis against the Pelagians. Thus it happens that men do not become faithful except by free will. And yet they become faithful by the Grace of Him who delivered their will from the power of darkness. Thus God's Grace is not denied, and free will is so defended that the will is made firm by humility, rather than being cast down by pride.

<summary>The author clarifies that his doctrine of free will is not heretical but aligns with St. Augustine’s teachings, emphasizing that while humans have the freedom to choose, they require God’s grace to act righteously.</summary>

This man was wounded and so weakened that he could not raise himself up by his own effort, but he could certainly consent and cooperate, within his meager power, with the charity of the Samaritan to be placed on his beast, carried to the inn, tended to, and healed. So it is with the <term>free will: the human capacity to choose between good and evil</term> of man; it is so wounded and weakened that by itself it cannot rise, and without the Grace of God it would remain in this lamentable state; but if God calls him, and stretches out His hand to him, he will answer His voice, and stretch out his own hand to Him, consenting and cooperating with the means of his healing. # Article XIV.

I had distinguished in this article the predestination of the wicked to the eternal pains of hell through the foreknowledge of their unbelief, impenitence, and bad works, and indicated the doctrine of Calvin and of Beza on this point: the former <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the primary figure of the Reformed tradition.</note> saying that *God has ORDAINED sin, and has no other reason for reprobation than His will*; and the latter <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), Calvin’s successor in Geneva.</note> saying that *God predestines to damnation and to its CAUSES*.

# Article XVI.

Having in this Article established the **justification** <term>justification: the process by which a sinner is made righteous before God</term> of man by **Faith** and by **works**, these Gentlemen say that the first part is true, and the second is false.

To properly understand this question, one must know that man, being wicked and unjust by himself, is justified by God through the absolution of his injustices and by the **infusion** <term>infusion: the theological idea that God "pours" grace and virtues directly into a person's soul</term> of virtues contrary to his vices—all through the merit of the death of Jesus Christ. By these means, *from being unjust, he is made just*, and becomes ever more just through the exercise of his justice, according to the sentence of the Lord Jesus: <margin>*Rev. 22:11*</margin> *Let the one who is righteous still be justified.* <note>original: "Qui est iuste se iustifie encore." The author uses this to argue that justification is an ongoing process of growth, not just a one-time event.</note>

This being established, it is easy to enlighten you on this subject. It is certain that St. Paul speaks to both Jews and Gentiles. The former believed they were justified by the works of the Law according to the promise: <margin>*Levit. 18:5*</margin> *do these things and you shall live.* And the latter believed they were justified by the good works of natural law, performed solely by the strength of their **free will**. They *think*, as St. Augustine says of the **Pelagians** <note>Pelagians: followers of Pelagius, a 5th-century theologian who taught that humans could achieve salvation through their own efforts without needing divine grace—a view the Catholic Church deemed heretical.</note>, *that they fulfill the commandments of the law by the strength of their own will, and in the entanglement of this pride, they do not turn toward Grace.*

It is clear that it is against them that this blessed <sig>I 3</sig> <meta>catchword: blessed</meta>

---

*St. Augustine, Book 3, Chapter 7, against the two Epistles of the Pelagians: They think that by the strength of their own free will they fulfill the law that commands it, and entangled in such pride, they do not turn to the grace that helps.* <note>original Latin: "Putant se arbitrij sui viribus implere legem iubentem, & ista implicati superbiâ ad gratiam non convertuntur iuuantem."</note>

As if I were to say: the inhabitants of St. John <note>Likely a reference to a local region known to the original readers, such as Saint-Jean-de-Luz.</note> cannot live on the fruits of their lands except by the blessing of Heaven; far from denying that they lived on the fruits of their lands, on the contrary, I would be affirming it, but I would necessarily be joining to it the blessing of Heaven. Thus St. Paul does not say in this passage that we are not justified by works, but he says that we are not justified without faith. And indeed, how could that be, since works without faith do nothing before God? And faith also without works is dead? Rather, it is living and made perfect by works, as St. James says. Learn from this how much you should trust the version of your Bibles.

<meta>This page continues the author’s theological defense of the Catholic doctrine of justification. Paul, as the common and true proverb says, nothing comes from nothing: These works, being given by grace and proceeding from grace, are freely given, and the <term>justification</term> <note>The theological process by which a sinner is made righteous in the eyes of God.</note> which is produced by them concurrently with faith is free, according to the maxim: *As the cause is, so is the effect.* And *what is the cause of the cause is the cause of the thing caused.* <note>original: "Qualis causa talis effectus. Et quod est causa causæ est causa causati." These are scholastic logic principles used here to argue that because God is the "cause" of our good works, the salvation those works bring is still ultimately "caused" by God.</note>

<margin>Ephesians 2:8</margin>
<margin>Hebrews 12:14</margin>
<margin>Matthew 16:27</margin>
<margin>Session 6, ch. 16 <note>Referring to the decrees of the Council of Trent.</note></margin>
It is otherwise with works done before grace, and by the sole strength of the <term>free will</term> <note>The human capacity to choose between different possible courses of action unimpeded.</note> of man. Of those works, St. Paul speaks when he says that one is not saved by them, so that he may not boast of them. But good works done in grace and by grace are conditions without which no adult is saved; for *without sanctification no one will see God*, says St. Paul. And He *will render to each according to his works*, says Jesus Christ; and those who receive the reward for them will have nothing to boast of in themselves, but in the Lord who produced the works in them, having been nothing but fellow-workers with Him. Thus, when crowning their works, He will not crown them as their own works, but as His own gifts—as the great St. Augustine so often says, and after him, the Council of Trent.

Until now, <insert>these gentlemen</insert> <note>A common polemical term for Protestant ministers or theologians whom the author is refuting.</note> have been unable to produce a single passage which says that man is justified by faith alone, or by faith only, except for the one in the second chapter of Galatians, which is manifestly falsified and which, in its original, does not say what they claim.

# Article XVII.

IN this Article, these Gentlemen <note>The author uses "these Gentlemen" as a polite but pointed reference to his Protestant opponents.</note> wander all over the place as is their habit, and contrary to the rules of formal debate—which prescribe making one's arguments short, concise, and touching upon the question at hand—they fill two or three pages without saying a single word relevant to the matter. I speak of the <term>potentiality</term> <note>original: "puissance." In Scholastic philosophy, this refers to the capacity or power to do something, as opposed to the actual doing of it.</note>, and they speak of the <term>act</term> <note>original: "acte." The actual performance of a deed.</note>, as if from the power to the deed there were not a "long road to travel," as the common saying goes.

<margin>James 3 <note>James 3:2: "For we all stumble in many things."</note>
Proverbs 20:9 <note>"Who can say, 'I have made my heart clean, I am pure from my sin'?"</note>
1 John 1:8 <note>"If we say that we have no sin, we deceive ourselves, and the truth is not in us."</note></margin>
The question is not at all whether we fulfill the law of God, for one would have to possess very little judgment not to know that *we all stumble in many things*, and there is not one who can say, *I am clean of sin*: for *he who says so would deceive himself, and the truth would not be in him*. What necessity, then, did these Gentlemen have to set themselves up as judges of my conscience to summon it, as they do, and ask if it fulfills the law of God perfectly? For they could well imagine that I would give them the same answer they would give themselves to a similar question.

Just as I must tell them that the fact that I do not fulfill it is not because it is impossible to fulfill by the assistance of God, to whom all things are possible; rather, it is my own failing in not cooperating and responding as I ought to that divine assistance in order to accomplish what I do not accomplish.

The comparison of the Spirit of God to a beautiful Soul within an infirm body, which does not perform all <note>The text ends mid-sentence as noted by the catchword "toutes" (all).</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's refutation of his opponents' arguments regarding the possibility of fulfilling God's law. ❦ it is madness, says St. Augustine, * *to hold a man guilty of sin for not having done what he was unable to do.*

I beg you to observe the methods of these Gentlemen <note>The author likely refers to the "Messieurs" of Port-Royal (Jansenists) or Calvinist theologians, with whom he is in a heated debate over the nature of grace and free will.</note> in three things. The first is that they fill two or three pages with passages from Scripture to prove that man does not fulfill the law—a point which no one has disputed with them. # Article XIX.

It is in this article above all that your **Gentlemen** <note>The author uses "Messieurs" (Gentlemen) to refer to Protestant ministers or critics of the Catholic Church.</note> wish to make your hair stand on end with horror regarding the fasts of the Catholic Church. And even though she <note>The Church.</note> supports them so well through **Holy Scripture** and the practice of the purest Church that has ever existed under Heaven, they make the constitutions she has established on this matter appear to your mind as things that are horrible, damnable, and ***diabolical***. They place her in the rank of those false **Judaizing** Christians <note>Judaizers were early Christians who insisted that the Laws of Moses, such as dietary restrictions and circumcision, were still mandatory for salvation.</note> from the time of the **Apostles**, and among the heretics of the following centuries, against whom those blessed servants of Jesus Christ had sharpened their sacred pens.

I have justified these fasts by the Scriptures of the **Old Testament** and the **New**, and by the practice of the **Apostolic** and **Christian Church**. These justifications are so clear that instead of opposing them with answers from Scripture or other authorities of an equal counterweight, they content themselves with passing over them in silence, not knowing how to offer any solid reply; they qualify all my proofs as a mere ***pretense*** <note>original: "semblant"</note>, as if I were not speaking in earnest, and as if only their "novelty" <note>The author uses "novelty" to describe the Reformed doctrines, contrasting them with the antiquity of Catholic tradition.</note> had discovered the precious pearl of truth.

To clarify this matter, I must inform you that the Catholic Church believes that all **meats** <term>viandes: in this period, the term could refer to food in general, but here specifically refers to animal flesh in the context of abstinence.</term> for the use of man are good and created by **God**, and that anyone who says the contrary—that some foods are pure and others impure—deserves to be **excommunicated** as a **heretic**. We do not abstain from any kind of meat because of the meat...

Rather, it is to further excite the spirit to piety, in proportion to how much one subdues the body by refusing it the things it desires.

<margin>Apostolic Canon 68 <note>The Apostolic Canons are a collection of ancient ecclesiastical decrees, once believed to have been written by the Apostles themselves.</note></margin>
The Canons attributed to them, and the testimony of the ancient Saints \*, certify that the *Lent comes from the Tradition and institution of the APOSTLES*, and has been religiously observed in the first and purest Centuries of Christianity; you will see these things at greater length in chapter 19 of my Italian book.

After this, are you surprised by the weak excuses of your Gentlemen <note>original: "vos Messieurs" — the author’s standard term for Protestant ministers.</note> regarding all these Sacred authorities? Are you surprised by the contempt they show for them, and by their boldness in wrapping all of Christendom for nearly 17 Centuries in a crime of such horror—the very one they have preached throughout this Article—and [their claim] that the spirit leading them is more intelligent and more holy than the one that has governed the Universal Church for so many hundreds of years?

Regarding the authorities I presented to them, they escape in this manner—which need only be reported to make you admit that they surrender their arms just when they act the most courageous: *All that he says,* they say, *and all that he alleges to the contrary does nothing for him, and only serves to waste paper uselessly.* If answering consists of speaking thus, what is there in the world so strong and formal that could not be eluded?

Yes, Gentlemen, here are all your Ministers who, to save their paper, answer nothing to the authority of the Apostolic and Christian Church of nearly seventeen hundred years, simply because it does not accord with the "harmony" of Calvin and their own which has existed for only a Century and a half. What Confusion!

## Article XX.

<margin>
Numbers 10:10.

**It is** in this Article that your Gentlemen Ministers <note>original: "Messieurs vos Ministres" — a polite but firm term the author uses for the local Protestant clergy.</note> rage no less against the *Roman Church* than in the preceding ones: For I had said that *the Roman Church celebrates, besides Sundays, solemn Feasts to glorify God for the wonders of His grace granted to His Church in general, and to His Saints in particular.* I gave them the reasons for this through the example of the Feasts of the *Old Testament* <note>original: "vieux Testament"</note> beyond the Sabbath to glorify God for the temporal favors granted to His *Israel*, and by the example of the Church of the first and purest Centuries of *Christianity*. They have not a word to say in response, and yet they call the Feasts of the Roman Church *an idolatry, an assault against God, and an enormous crime* without any other proof from *Scripture* than because it seems so to them.

<margin>
Exodus 32:5.
</margin>
They first fall into a great inconsistency: for wishing to prove that the Feasts of the Angels and Saints constitute *idolatry*, they allege the idolatry of Israel in celebrating a Feast in honor of the *Golden Calf*.

<margin>
Verse 5.
</margin>
Firstly, they contradict *Scripture*, which does not say that the Feast was made *to the Golden Calf*, but *to the Eternal* <note>original: "l'Eternel" — a common French term for the Tetragrammaton (the name of God), often used in Protestant translations of the Bible.</note>; *Aaron* cried out, *Tomorrow there shall be a solemn Feast to the Eternal*, says the Text. Your Gentlemen cannot say that one commits the crime of idolatry by making a Feast to the *Eternal*, because since the *Eternal* is not an idol, to render him *latria* <term>Latria: from the Greek *latreia*, this refers to the supreme worship and adoration due to God alone, as opposed to the honor shown to saints.</term> through a Feast is not to commit the *crime of idolatry*; rather, it is a holy thing to celebrate Feasts to the *Eternal* when it is to glorify Him, as my Article says.

<term>Gentlemen</term><note>The author uses "Messieurs" to address the Protestant ministers or leaders with whom he is in theological conflict.</note>, although the Church dedicates certain days to <term>creatures</term><note>In this theological context, "creatures" refers to any created being, including human Saints and Angels, as opposed to the Creator (God).</note>, she knows very well that God alone is their Author and Master. Let these Gentlemen say whatever they wish against the feasts of the Roman Church, calling them *an idolatry*, *an attack against God*, and *an enormous crime*; yet, as long as they do not speak this way of the feasts of their brothers in England <note>A reference to the Church of England, which, unlike the more radical Calvinist Reformers, retained many traditional feast days in its calendar.</note>, they can never deny (without opposing the truth) that passion leads them more than reason in this matter. For the Roman Church, just like the English, dedicates these days only to God. Whether the litanies <note>A form of prayer consisting of a series of petitions.</note> are addressed only to God, or also to the Saints, does not affect this specific question.

Just as God did not forbid, in the feasts of the Old Testament which were dedicated to Him alone, the making of a memory at the same time of Moses, Aaron, Esther, Mordecai, and others, to renew the honor due to their memory; so too, it is not forbidden in the feasts of the Christian Church to celebrate the virtues and actions of its Saints, and to recommend their remembrance, honor, and imitation.

*Lord*. <note>The author uses the word "Prêtres" (Priests). In the Catholic tradition, this passage is the primary scriptural basis for the Sacrament of the Anointing of the Sick. Protestant reformers generally translated the Greek term "presbyteros" as "elder" rather than "priest."</note> Here Scripture formally commands 
*this anointing of the sick*, and the Ministers <note>The term used by the author to refer to Protestant clergy, often in a slightly dismissive or distinguishing tone.</note> forbid 
it. Whom shall we obey? The former is *divinely* 
<margin>2 Timothy 3:16</margin> *inspired*, but of these Gentlemen, who 
can assure us of their [inspiration]?

They object that the Holy Apostles did not anoint, 
nor recommend anointing the sick *except for* 
*their healing*: And yet the Apostles teach you that 
this was so that *if the sick person had committed* 
*sins, they would be forgiven him*.

They respond that this forgiveness of sins is 
not attributed to the anointing with Oil, but to prayer: 
*And the prayer made in faith shall raise up the sick, and if he* 
*has committed sins they shall be forgiven him*: but if 
this response is valid, it must also be said that 
 the raising up of the sick cannot be attributed to 
the anointing, but to prayer: *the prayer made in faith shall raise* 
*up the sick*, says the Apostle. Now this is directly 
contrary to what they have just objected: 
that this anointing was performed only for healing.

It must therefore be said that the forgiveness of 
sins is as much the effect of the anointing as is the healing. And 
consequently, since there are always sins to be forgiven 
until the end of the world, just as there are illnesses to 
be healed, until the end of the world one must also 
anoint the sick with oil, and pray for them in faith; this 
Holy Apostle does not restrict the time of the one 
any more than the other, but joins the one 
inseparably to the other!

This foundation which they had laid with such 
falsehood being ruined by these incontestable 
proofs

Since Holy Scripture is so clear on this point <note>Referring to the biblical basis for anointing the sick, particularly in the Epistle of James.</note>, and the Universal Church for nearly seventeen hundred years has always practiced this Extreme Unction for the sick without any interruption, it is a very great audacity for these Gentlemen to call it ridiculous, and to persevere in the abolition carried out by Waldo, Luther, and Calvin, based on pretexts for which none of them can produce a single iota from the word of God, nor from the Tradition of the Church.

# Article XXIII.

<margin>Matthew 16:18</margin>
<margin>Hebrews 10 <note>Specifically referring to Hebrews 10:27 regarding the "fearful expectation of judgment."</note></margin>
<margin>Philippians 2:13</margin>
You Gentlemen are no more successful in the oppositions you have raised against this Article than in those you raised against the previous ones.

They first report the sentence of their condemnation that the Council of Trent <note>An ecumenical council of the Catholic Church (1545–1563) held to clarify doctrine in response to the Protestant Reformation.</note> pronounced against them, and the anathema <note>A formal curse by a church leader, excommunicating a person or denouncing a doctrine.</note> it justly declared against them for having opposed this truth. They are right to call it *terrible*, for the excommunications and anathemas of the Church are *sentences bound in Heaven as they are on earth* <note>A reference to the "power of the keys" granted to Peter in Matthew 16:19.</note>, and as these Gentlemen continue to oppose them, as they do, there remains for them only a *TERRIBLE expectation of judgment*, unless they acknowledge their error. I wish that it may be so terrible to them and to you that, fearing it as you ought, you might all work toward your *salvation with fear and trembling*.

Holy Scripture is the certain rule of our Faith, and for that reason, I believe this Article to be true because it conforms to the Scripture, which proves that the five [sacraments] contested by our adversaries are Sacraments in the same manner that it proves Baptism and the Eucharist <note>Protestants generally recognize only two sacraments (Baptism and the Lord's Supper), while Catholics recognize seven (adding Confirmation, Penance, Anointing of the Sick, Holy Orders, and Matrimony).</note>. I have proven it in this Article by showing that the definition of a Sacrament applies to the five contested ones just as it does to the two acknowledged ones. And that is the justice I do for these Gentlemen and for you: if you reread my summary, you will see them proven not only by Holy Scripture but by orthodox Antiquity <note>The practices and beliefs of the early Christian Church.</note>, and by [the words of] the opponents themselves, and especially by the figures raised up in an extraordinary manner to fight against this truth <note>The author likely refers ironically to the Protestant Reformers, suggesting their own arguments or internal contradictions inadvertently support Catholic doctrine.</note>.

# Concerning Confirmation.

These Gentlemen <note>Referring to the Protestant ministers or theologians whose arguments the author is refuting.</note> demand that I show them the institution of the five contested Sacraments, and their administration by the Apostles as signs of the covenant.
I show them these things in this one <note>The Sacrament of Confirmation.</note>. Its institution is apparent through its execution, which is represented to us in Acts 8 and 19; the Apostles would not have done this if it had not been commanded of them by Jesus Christ, and if they had not been led by the Spirit of God, by whom they were guided and animated. This is so true that Calvin <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), a leading French reformer who generally rejected Confirmation as a sacrament, though the author here highlights a point of partial agreement.</note> cannot deny it.
<margin>2 Timothy 4:7</margin>
If to this you add that the Apostles, by their laying on of hands, did what Jesus Christ himself did, as is seen in the 19th chapter of Saint Matthew. This laying on of hands—being a visible sign of the graces of the Holy Spirit, strengthening and con-firming <note>The author uses the word "confirmant" to emphasize the etymological and spiritual link to the name of the Sacrament.</note> the faithful to fight the good fight, keep the faith, and finish the race—is a sign of the graces of the covenant and of the grace necessary to remain loyal in this covenant until the end. This laying on of hands was not always for the purpose of conferring the gift of miracles: for what miracle did the children perform upon whom Jesus Christ laid his hands? And what miracles were performed by the millions of Christians who received this laying on of hands during the first four centuries of Christianity?

---

*Institutes 4. 19. 5. 6. They indeed defend themselves by the example of the Apostles, whom they believe to have done nothing rashly. That is certainly right.* <note>original: "Tuuntur se quidem Apostolorum exemplo, quos nihil temerè fecisse existimant. Id sanè rectè." This citation from Calvin's Institutes of the Christian Religion is used by the author to show that even Calvin acknowledged the Apostles acted under divine guidance.</note>

<summary>The author defends the Sacrament of Confirmation by arguing that its practice by the Apostles in the New Testament proves it was instituted by Christ. He contends that the laying on of hands provides necessary spiritual strength for the Christian life, rather than being solely for performing miracles.</summary>
<keywords>Confirmation, Sacraments, Apostles, Covenant, Holy Spirit, John Calvin, Laying on of Hands, Acts of the Apostles, 2 Timothy</keywords>

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the Sacrament of Confirmation. He is responding to the Protestant argument that the sacrament was a late invention of the medieval Church, specifically addressing a citation from the scholastic theologian Alexander of Hales.</meta>

These Gentlemen <note>The author’s Protestant opponents.</note> object to me by citing Alexander of Hales <note>Alexander of Hales (c. 1185–1245), an influential Franciscan theologian and philosopher.</note>, saying that the Sacrament of Confirmation *as a Sacrament was not instituted by the Lord, nor by the Apostles, but was since instituted at the Council of Meaux.* <note>The Council of Meaux was held in 845 AD.</note>

They say that these words are found in the second member of the twenty-fourth question of the fourth part, where certainly he does not say a word of all that; and these Gentlemen will never make me read it in that place, for he treats there of the *necessity of satisfaction* <term>satisfaction: in Catholic theology, the reparative action performed to amend for the temporal punishment of sin</term>, a subject quite different from the Confirmation in question here.

And assuming that he did say that in some other place than the one they cited to me, I reply that he ought not to have said it.

1. Because the Council says clearly in Canons 44, 45, and 46 that the *Vice-Bishops*, whom it calls *Chorepiscopi* <term>chorepiscopi: from the Greek "country-bishops"; auxiliary bishops who served rural areas</term>, must not give the *Holy Chrism* <term>Holy Chrism: a mixture of oil and balsam consecrated by a bishop and used in the administration of sacraments like Confirmation</term>, *which belongs only to the Bishops according to the institution of the Canons and the decrees of Pope Innocent.* It forbids the Bishops from taking any gift, whether of money or other things, for the administration of this Holy Chrism, and commands them to do it only on the Thursday of Holy Week. This, far from establishing this Sacrament as a Sacrament only at that time, presupposes that it already existed beforehand.

---

*Alexander of Hales, part 4, question 24, member 2.* **The Sacrament of Confirmation, as it is a Sacrament, was instituted neither by the Lord nor the Apostles: but was later instituted in the Council of Meaux.** <note>original: "Sacramentum Confirmationis ut est Sacramentum, neq; Dominus instituit, neque Apostoli : Sed postea institutum est in Concilio Meldenfi."</note>

*Concerning the necessity of satisfaction through external work.* <note>original: "De necessitate satisfactionis per opus exterius."</note>

*Canon 44.* That the Chorepiscopus <note>a regional assistant bishop</note> keep his own measure according to Canonical Institution, and attempt to hand over neither the Holy Chrism nor the Holy Spirit the Comforter, which is to be granted by Bishops alone according to the decrees of Innocent; nor should he consecrate churches, etc. <note>original: "Ut Chorepiscopus modum suum iuxta Canonicam Institutionem teneat, & nec Sanctum Chrisma, nec Sanctum Paracletum Spiritum solis Episcopis iuxta decreta Innocentij tribuere debitum tradere tentet, nec Ecclesias consecret &c."</note>

<summary>The author refutes the claim that Confirmation was invented at the 9th-century Council of Meaux by arguing that the council's regulations on the sacrament prove its prior existence.</summary>
<keywords>Sacrament of Confirmation, Alexander of Hales, Council of Meaux, Chorepiscopi, Holy Chrism, Pope Innocent, scholasticism, ecclesiastical law</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s refutation of the idea that the Sacrament of Confirmation was a late medieval invention. Having previously addressed a specific citation from Alexander of Hales, the author now provides evidence from early Church history to prove the sacrament's apostolic origins.</meta>

2. Because the opposite of what the said Alexander of Hales <note>Alexander of Hales (c. 1185–1245) was a prominent Franciscan theologian; the author argues that historical evidence predating him contradicts the Protestant claims.</note> says is established by testimonies much older than he, who died only in the year 1245.

St. Clement, living in the year 91, exhorts all the baptized to hasten to receive the *confirmation* of the Bishop, as the Christians had learned from St. Peter, and as the other Apostles had ordained by the COMMANDMENT OF THE LORD.

Pope Fabian—elected because of the Dove that came to rest upon him during the election of the Pontiff, and a Martyr for Christ in the year 251—says that the Lord, on the evening after the supper, and after having washed the feet of His Disciples, ordained that the Holy Chrism <term>Holy Chrism: a consecrated mixture of oil and balsam used in several sacraments</term> be renewed every year, as had been learned from the Holy Apostles and their Successors; and since the Apostles, these things have been observed in Rome, Antioch, Jerusalem, and Ephesus.

---

Canon 45. That no Bishop, nor any Ecclesiastical Minister, shall receive any gift for the Holy Chrism, neither money nor any small gifts which are improperly demanded from priests by the ministers of Bishops, etc. <note>original Latin: "Ut nemo Episcoporum, vel quilibet Mininiſter Ecclesiasticus propter Sacrum Chrisma aliquid muneris accipiat..."</note>

Canon 46. That no one shall presume to prepare the Holy Chrism except on the Thursday of the Great Week <note>Holy Thursday</note>, that is, at the Supper which is specifically called the Lord's [Supper]. <note>original Latin: "Ut nemo Sacrum Chrisma nisi in quintâ feriâ maioris septimanæ, idest in Cœnâ quæ specialiter appellatur Dominica, conficere præsumat."</note>

<margin>Epistle 4</margin>
St. Clement: Therefore, all must hasten without delay to be reborn to God, and finally to be signed by the Bishop, and to receive the sevenfold grace <note>Referring to the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit: wisdom, understanding, counsel, fortitude, knowledge, piety, and fear of the Lord.</note> of the Holy Spirit, as we have received from Blessed Peter, and as the other Holy Apostles established by the COMMAND OF THE LORD. <note>original Latin: "Omnibus ergo festinandum est sine mora renasci Deo, & demùm consignari ab Episcopo, & septiformem gratiam Sp. S. percipere..."</note>

<meta>This page continues the author’s presentation of Patristic evidence for the Sacrament of Confirmation, moving from the 1st and 2nd centuries into the 3rd and 4th centuries to further challenge the claims of Alexander of Hales.</meta>

St. Cyprian, Bishop of Carthage and a martyr for Christ in the year 250, says *that one can only then be fully sanctified and be a child of God if one is born of the TWO SACRAMENTS* <note>St. Cyprian (c. 210–258) refers here to the "double sacrament" of Baptism and the laying on of hands (Confirmation), arguing they are two parts of a single process of Christian initiation.</note>.

Pope Miltiades <note>also called Melchiades, Pope from 311–314</note>, living in the year 311 and also speaking of Baptism and Confirmation, says, *know that both are GREAT SACRAMENTS*.

St. Augustine, living in the fourth century of the Church, does not fail to give us his faithful testimony, saying that the *SACREMENT of the Chrism is MOST HOLY, JUST AS BAPTISM IS*.

Place, Gentlemen <note>The author is likely addressing Protestant ministers or a group of Reformers</note>, all these testimonies from those who were the most learned, the most holy, and the most authentic in the first centuries of the Church in the balance against the saying of Alexander of Hales, and you will see that your leaders set aside what carries weight in order to peddle something to you that cannot counter-balance it.

<meta>catchword: Here</meta>

---

*St. Fabian, Epistle 2 to the Bishops of the East.* <note>original Latin: "S. Fab. Ep. 2. ad Episcopos Orientis. In illâ die Dominus Iesus postquàm cœnauit cum Discipulis suis & lauit eorum pedes sicut à Sanctis Apostolis Prædecessores nostri acceperunt, nobísque reliquerunt, Chrisma conficere docuit..."</note> On that day, the Lord Jesus, after he had supped with his disciples and washed their feet—as our predecessors received from the Holy Apostles and left to us—taught how to prepare the Chrism. Thus we have received from the Holy Apostles and their successors. This the Holy Roman and Antiochian Church has kept since the time of the Apostles, etc.

*St. Cyprian, Book 2, Epistle 1.* <note>original Latin: "S. Cypr. l. 2. Ep. 1. Tunc demùm plenè sanctificari & esse filij Dei possunt si sacramento utroque nascantur."</note> *Then at last can they be fully sanctified and be children of God, if they be born of both sacraments.*

*Miltiades, Epistle to the Bishops of Spain.* <note>original Latin: "Melchiad. Ep. ad Episcopos Hisp. Scitote utrumq; magnum esse Sacramentum."</note> *Know that both are a great sacrament.*

*St. Augustine, Book 2, Against the Letters of Petilian the Donatist, Chapter 104.* <note>original Latin: "S. Aug. l. 2. c. lit. Pet. Don. c 104. Sacramentum Chrismatis in genere visibilium signaculorum sacrosanctum est sicut ipse baptismus."</note> *The sacrament of the Chrism is, in the category of visible signs, sacrosanct just as baptism itself is.*

<summary>The author cites early Church Fathers—including Cyprian, Miltiades, and Augustine—to argue that Confirmation has been considered a major sacrament since the early centuries. <meta>This page continues the author’s argument for the antiquity and necessity of the Sacrament of Confirmation. Here, the modern Catholic Church is engaged in the same war with our separated brethren <note>original: "nos separés"; a 17th-century term for Protestants</note> of this time as the Catholic Church of the time of the same St. Augustine had against the Novatians and Donatists <note>The Novatians and Donatists were early Christian groups who broke away from the main church over strict views on purity and church discipline; they were often cited in later polemics to label contemporary groups as schismatic</note> who revolted against its communion. This is shown by this most faithful witness of the faith and common practice of those centuries, who, because of their heresy, called their heart a "perverse heart."

"*Does any of you*," he said to them, "*look to see if the children upon whom hands are laid speak in tongues? And seeing that they do not speak in tongues, has any of you said that they have not received the Holy Spirit?*" <note>St. Augustine is arguing that the internal grace of the Holy Spirit in Confirmation does not require the outward miraculous sign of "speaking in tongues" as it did in the days of the Apostles.</note>

There, Gentlemen, is the praise that this Holy Doctor <note>A title for eminent theologians like St. Augustine</note> gives you through the persona of those heretics who spoke just as your Ministers do.

<margin>Anglican Liturgy</margin>
The English observe Confirmation through their Bishops every three years, and they say that it is *of Apostolic institution and of the ancient usage of the Church*, calling the abolition of it by their brethren in other countries sacrilegious. Ask your Ministers for a moment: where are their Bishops? And why do they not observe this *Apostolic Institution*? And why do they persevere in its sacrilegious abolition? They cannot give you a true answer, except that this is not how Waldo <note>Peter Waldo (c. 1140–1218), founder of the Waldensians, an early reforming group</note> and Calvin <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the French reformer whose theology shaped the Presbyterian and Reformed churches</note> reformed them.

# On Penance.

The Gentlemen Ministers <note>A common 17th-century Catholic term for Protestant pastors, particularly those of the Reformed/Calvinist tradition.</note> claim that penance is not a Sacrament because it lacks a material element <note>In theology, a "material element" (materia) refers to the physical substance used in a sacrament, such as water in Baptism or bread and wine in the Eucharist.</note>, presupposing as true that which is very false: that nothing can be a Sacrament where there is no physical element. For Circumcision was a sacrament, and yet where is its material element?

Calvin admits that the laying on of hands in legitimate ordinations is a Sacrament, and yet where is the material element there? St. Augustine, speaking of penance under the name of *reconciliation*, calls it a *Sacrament*; *The cause of baptism and of reconciliation is the same, without which SACRAMENTS men believe they should not depart from the Body* <note>original Latin: "Eadem est causa baptismi & reconciliationis, sine quibus SACREMENS les hommes croient ne devoir sortir du Corps." The "Body" here refers to the physical body, meaning one should not die without these sacraments.</note>.

Absolution justifies man and truly forgives him his sin: but not without contrition of the heart and confession of the mouth, if it is possible. It does not, however, cease to be a visible sign of the grace of God, and consequently a Sacrament, just as the Eucharist is a Sacrament even if the Communicant does not bring the necessary dispositions to that action—namely faith, repentance, and charity.

---

*St. Augustine, Book 1 on Adulterous Marriages, chapters 26 and 28. The cause of baptism and of reconciliation is the same, without which sacraments men believe they should not depart from the body.* <note>original Latin: "S. Aug. l. 1. de adulter. coniug. c. 26. 28. Eadem est causa baptismi & reconciliationis, sine quibus Sacramentis homines credunt se non debere exire de corpore."</note>

*St. Augustine, Tractate 6 on the Epistle of St. John. Someone was of such a perverse heart, etc.* <note>original Latin: "S. Aug. tract. 6. in Epist. S. Ioh. Ita perverso corde aliquis fuit, &c."</note>

### On Holy Orders.

In this point, the disorder of your Gentlemen <note>The author refers to the Protestant "Ministers" or pastors he is debating.</note> is revealed: they call their writing an *Examination of the motives that led me to change my Religion*. This topic is one of those motives, and instead of examining it, they do as they do in most other cases: they propose their maxims according to the dictate of Calvin and his other companions and conformists, without answering a single word to the reasons and motives that obliged me to change my Religion regarding this Article.

<margin>1 Timothy 4:14.</margin>
I had said that [Holy] Order is a Sacrament, because it is a visible sign of the Grace of God according to what St. Paul says to Timothy: *Do not neglect the Grace that is in you, and was given to you by the laying on of hands* <note>The "laying on of hands" is the ritual act used in ordination to signify the conferring of spiritual authority.</note>.

I show the brilliance of this truth through the Church of the Golden Ages <note>The "Golden Ages" refers to the early centuries of the Christian Church, often cited by both Catholics and Protestants as the standard of pure doctrine.</note> according to the testimony of its most faithful witness*, who says that NO ONE DOUBTS that it is a SACRAMENT just like Baptism. I have even brought forward the admission of Calvin, your Reformer. And these Gentlemen answer nothing, neither to the passage from St. Paul, nor to the testimony of St. Augustine, nor to the admission of Calvin: but finding this bone too hard, they did not dare to undertake it, and by their silence, they plead guilty. And indeed, what can they answer to three authorities as clear and strong as those, and which are the pillars upon which they usually pretend to rest their Reformation?

<meta>catchword: Everything</meta>

<note>original Latin: "S. Aug. l. 2. c. Parm c. 13. Si vtrumque Sacramentum est [ scil. Bapt. & Ordo ] quod nemo dubitat."</note>

Everything they say afterward is of their own particular invention, without any foundation in the word of God. For who told them that [Holy] Order is not a Sacred Sign of God's covenant in Jesus Christ, since it is a Sacred Sign of the Grace of God which is given because of this covenant, and is an effect of it? Who told them that it is not a Sacrament because it is not common to all the faithful? Was <term>Circumcision</term> <note>The ritual removal of the foreskin, which in the Old Testament served as a sign of the covenant between God and the people of Israel.</note> not a Sacrament even though it was not common to women? Who told them that it does not confer the remission of sins? If the Grace of God, which is given through this means, does not confer this remission of sins, what will confer it? What passage, finally, do they produce for you from the Old Testament which says that Ordination was given by the <term>laying on of hands</term> <note>The physical act of a bishop placing hands on a candidate to confer spiritual authority and grace.</note>, and conferred the Grace of God along with it? How long, O poor people, will you listen to such doctrines? This is not what St. Paul taught you; nor is it what the Church taught until the time when *nothing had changed since the Apostles*: Its very good Witness* testified to this to you before, and he testifies to it again, saying: *The bond of marriage is not dissolved except by death, just as the SACRAMENT of ORDERS remains in those who are ordained*. If this Doctor, whom your Ministers so often call *Great and Holy*, were alive today, how could he speak in such a way and be a Minister of these Valleys? Your <term>Synod</term> <note>A formal meeting of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine and administration.</note> would undoubtedly not tolerate him in its Registry: likewise, I am certain he would not have signed their *Examination*.

## On Marriage.

These Gentlemen <note>A polite but firm reference to the Protestant ministers the author is debating.</note> do not want Marriage to be a Sacrament: 1. because it was not instituted by Jesus Christ under the **Gospel**, but by God at the beginning of the world. They pretend to be unaware that Marriage is considered in two ways: as a **Civil Contract** and the joining of male and female for the propagation of the human race; and as a sacred sign of the union of Christ with his Church.

In the first respect, it was instituted from the beginning, and was continued among the Jews and the **Gentiles** <note>Non-Jewish peoples of the ancient world.</note> until Jesus Christ, and still is among the infidels <note>In this context, those outside the Christian faith.</note>.

In the second respect, it is considered under the Gospel, as **St. Paul** teaches in the 5th chapter of his **Epistle to the Ephesians**, where speaking of Marriage he calls it *a great mystery* <note>original: "vn grand mistere." The Greek word *mysterion* used by Paul was translated into Latin as *sacramentum*, forming the biblical basis for the Catholic view of marriage as a sacrament.</note> in reference to Christ and his Church, because it is a sign of a sacred thing, which is the union of Christ with his Church. Now, this union is achieved through grace; it follows that this sign is also a moral instrument of the Grace of God
<margin>John 17.</margin>
by which we are made *one with him as he is one with the Father*, and just as the husband and the wife
<margin>Matthew 19.</margin>
*shall no longer be two, but one flesh*: so that *what is thus joined together, let no one separate*.

2. They do not want Marriage to be a Sacrament because there is no element <note>In theology, an "element" is the material substance used in a sacrament, such as water in Baptism or bread and wine in the Eucharist. The author is beginning to address the Protestant critique that Marriage lacks such a physical component.</note>; but to

He uses the writings of St. Paul and the Gospels to argue that marriage conveys divine grace.</summary>

And it has its sensible and visible sign <note>In theology, a "sensible sign" is something that can be perceived by the senses (like words or a ring) which points to an invisible spiritual reality.</note> which is the consent of the parties testified by some word or external token.

<margin>1 Timothy 2:15</margin>
If all those who marry in the Church do not unite themselves to Jesus Christ by means of carnal marriage, that does not prevent it from being a Sacrament. So it is with Marriage: not all married people acquire this union with Christ; however, Marriage is—by the admission of your own Ministers in their *Examination*—a type <note>A "type" is a person, thing, or event in the Old Testament or in nature believed to foreshadow something in the New Testament—in this case, the relationship between Christ and the Church.</note> of the mystical union of Jesus Christ with his Church. If it is a type, why is it not a sign, a seal, and a Sacrament, being an external ceremony that has the promise of Grace annexed to it? * "The man and the woman will be saved through the generation of children if they remain in faith, love, and sanctification with modesty." <note>A reference to 1 Timothy 2:15, though the author expands the subject to both spouses.</note>

Gentlemen, your Ministers draw a Conclusion from all their reasons, namely that the **Fathers of the Church** call Marriage a Sacrament in a broad sense, and not in a proper and strict sense. What I have said above shows the invalidity of these reasons. Therefore, the Conclusion they draw from them is void, as it is easy to show you. It is certain that Baptism is a Sacrament, not in a broad sense, but in a proper and strict sense. Now

Marriage is a Sacrament just like Baptism. It is therefore a Sacrament in its proper meaning. <note>In theology, a "proper meaning" or "strict sense" refers to a rite that is specifically instituted by Christ to confer grace, as opposed to a "broad sense" which might refer to any holy sign.</note>

I prove that it is a Sacrament just like Baptism, because it is a Sacrament like Holy Orders, as St. Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD) was a highly influential bishop and theologian whose writings form the backbone of much Western Christian thought.</note> has said previously; and according to this Holy Father, Holy Orders is a Sacrament just like Baptism—as you have seen above—even going so far as to say that there is no one who doubts it. Therefore, according to this Holy Father, Marriage is also a Sacrament, not in a broad sense, as these Gentlemen <note>The author uses "these Gentlemen" as a polite but firm way to refer to the Protestant ministers or reformers he is debating.</note> say, but in a proper and strict meaning, just like Baptism.

## Article XXIV.

Regarding the report these Gentlemen make on the four headings proposed in this Article, they do not act in good faith. In the first, I had said that the Roman Church teaches that Baptism is so necessary for salvation that, *as far as the ordinary means revealed to us by Scripture are concerned, we cannot affirm that those who die without it go to Paradise*. They leave behind these words: *as far as the ordinary means revealed to us by Scripture are concerned.* <note>The author makes a crucial theological distinction: while God can save through "extraordinary" means (His absolute power), the "ordinary" way prescribed in the Bible is through the Sacrament of Baptism.</note>

In the second, I had said that the Roman Church recognizes that for this purpose, in cases of necessity, it is permitted in every place, at every time, and for all sorts of people to administer this Sacrament. And they omit the words, *in every place, at every time*, intentionally keeping silent about what troubles them, and making up the thesis themselves as they please to follow

but [to prove] by Scripture that it is commanded to baptize little children:

That, assuming it were commanded, they are wrong to baptize them only in the places and public exercises of the Sermon, or evening and morning prayer, as their Discipline prescribes: seeing that the commandment to baptize does not limit the time of its administration: so that if the child is about to die at noon, or at midnight outside the hours of their public exercises, it is not permitted by their discipline to obey the commandment of God to baptize, even though they say it is *just that He should be obeyed*.

And if every person who refuses baptism deprives themselves of salvation, as they say, then that Minister deprives himself of it who, to obey his Discipline, refuses to give it to a dying child outside the hours of public evening and morning prayers.

Having admitted that it is necessary by the necessity of commandment, they deny that it is necessary by the <term>necessity of means<note>A theological distinction: a "necessity of commandment" means something must be done because God ordered it; a "necessity of means" means the act itself is the essential instrument through which God grants salvation.</note></term>, as if the baptism commanded by God were not a means of salvation. The decree of **Jesus Christ** is without any exception: *Unless one is born of water and spirit, he cannot enter the Kingdom of God*. This is a point which the **Lutherans** and **Diodati** <note>Giovanni Diodati (1576–1649), a Protestant theologian whose Bible translation was highly influential among French-speaking Reformed churches.</note>—the interpreter of the Bible whom the Ministers follow when explaining the texts they choose for their sermons—understand to refer as much to the <term>baptism of water<note>original: batéme d'eau</note></term> as to the inner regeneration or sanctification of man.

One cannot argue that the circumcised Nicodemus, nor the converted thief on the cross, were deprived of salvation. This is partly because baptism was not yet obligatory before the full proclamation of the Gospel following the Resurrection and Ascension of the Lord, and partly because we do not know for certain whether Nicodemus was ever baptized.

<margin>Mark 10:39.</margin>
Furthermore, one cannot say that the converted Thief was not baptized in his desire, faith, and repentance. This also applies to those who have made a vow to be baptized, or those who received the Holy Spirit before the sacrament: for that very Spirit moves them to desire and receive it. This also includes the martyrs, whose <term>martyrdom<note>original: martire</note></term> is itself a baptism, which compensates for the baptism of water according to the testimony of two great Saints, namely **St. Cyprian** and **St. Augustine**. The former says: *Can the power of baptism be greater or more effective than confession and suffering?* <note>original: "Nunquid potest vis Baptismi esse maior aut potior quàm confessio, quàm passio?" from Cyprian's Letter to Jubaianus.</note> And the latter opens his mouth with these words: *Likewise, all those who die for the confession of Christ without having received the washing...*

**St. Cyprian**, Letter to Jubaianus. *Can the power of Baptism be greater or more effective than confession, than suffering?*

**St. Augustine**, *The City of God*, Book 13, Chapter 7. *Whosoever dies for the confession of Christ, even if they have not received the washing of regeneration, it is as effective for them for the forgiveness of sins as if they were washed in the sacred font of baptism.*

<meta>This page continues the author's polemic against Protestant ministers (referred to as "these Gentlemen") regarding the administration of baptism. He is currently refuting their claim that only those ordained to preach the Gospel have the authority to baptize, arguing that such a logic would lead to the errors of the Anabaptists or contradict historical examples of lay teaching.</meta>

If the argument of these Gentlemen is good, that of the Anabaptists is as well. <note>The Anabaptists were a radical wing of the Reformation who believed that baptism should only be administered to believing adults, thereby rejecting infant baptism.</note> **Jesus Christ** commands to baptize those whom He commands to instruct. Therefore, one must only baptize those who must be instructed.

There is an argument similar to theirs. And yet it is entirely false, because it excludes small children from **Baptism**, who cannot be instructed because of their age.

<margin>
Acts 18: 2, 3, 26.
</margin>
But it is right that I strike these Gentlemen with Scripture & by their own weapons: if it is only those who have the charge to instruct who can baptize, and yet in cases of necessity, persons who do not have this charge have instructed—like the tent-makers *Aquila* & *Priscilla* his wife, who explained the way of God more perfectly to *Apollos*. <note>This biblical reference highlights that laypeople (non-clergy) were capable of teaching doctrine in the early Church.</note>

And previously these Gentlemen mentioned to us *Oedesius* & *Frumentius*, who were only locksmiths, and yet planted the Gospel in *the Indies*; <note>Historical traditions identify these two as laymen who brought Christianity to the Kingdom of Aksum (modern-day Ethiopia), which was often called "the Indies" in ancient and early modern texts.</note> of *Maturianus* & *Saturnianus*, who were only poor slaves, and yet advanced Christianity in *Mauritania*: <note>Ancient North Africa.</note> These people had no ordinary charge to instruct, and yet they instructed in cases of necessity; And if they instructed, who will assure me that they did not also baptize?
