I use a similar reason for the proof of this truth taken from *Waldo*, a Merchant of *Lyon*, from whom histories call you sometimes *Waldensians*, sometimes *Lionois*, <note>Reference to Peter Waldo, the 12th-century founder of the Waldensian movement, often viewed by later Protestants as spiritual ancestors.</note> & I say that either he or his *Barbes* <term>Barbes</term> <note>From the Occitan 'barba' meaning uncle; the term used for the pastors or elders of the Waldensian community.</note> among the *Waldensians* had at that time let the inhabitants of these Valleys die without **Baptism** (which you would not dare to say, for it would have been too enormous a crime not to obey the Commandment of **Jesus**...

On this subject, I had said that if an **Elder** distributes the Cup at their **Lord's Supper**, and they hold that this Cup forms but one **Sacrament** with the bread, then an **Elder**—who does not have the duty of teaching doctrine—in administering the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper, can also administer **Baptism** in cases of necessity.

This reasoning carries such weight that they have only been able to respond with frivolous things, the falsity of which you yourselves are witnesses.

*"It is not the Elder,"* they say, *"who consecrates, but the Minister."* But if I make you touch with your own finger <note>A French idiom meaning to prove something so clearly it is as if it were physically tangible.</note> that it is neither one nor the other, what will you say?

It is not the **Elder** who consecrates, I admit: for when he hands the glass to those passing by <note>Referring to the congregants filing past to receive communion.</note>, he does not say a word. It is not the **Minister** either, for he says nothing regarding this Cup; he does not consecrate it then, as everyone knows. He does not consecrate it in the **Pulpit**, for he only reads the institution of this **Sacrament**, the teachings on its meaning, and the exhortations to prepare for its reception—which cannot be called **Consecration**, as he himself acknowledges, since he claims to consecrate anew by the words he utters while giving the bread:
> *Take, eat; this is the Body of Christ broken for you.*

<margin>Luke 22:17</margin>
If Our Lord said to his Disciples, *take the Cup and distribute it among yourselves*, it is because they were teaching the nations—which an **Elder** does not do—and because He had established them as administrators of His **Sacrament** by the words *do this*, and because He had consecrated it before giving it to them; this is something the **Minister** does not do, as he does not consecrate the Cup that the **Elder** distributes.

We know that even **Laypeople** are permitted to baptize if necessity constrains them.

And it is in this case of necessity that the Church says **Laypeople** can baptize, and not within the Church or publicly, nor even in private in the presence of ecclesiastical persons.

<margin>*Roman Ritual, p. 9.*</margin>
It is in this sense that **Tertullian** * says that *it is not permitted for a woman to speak in the Church, to teach, nor to baptize*, (add to that *to offer* <note>original: "offrir"; referring to the offering of the Eucharist</note>, which these Gentlemen <note>The author's Protestant opponents.</note> omit on purpose to hide the truth). For it is clear that this refers to the public ministry of the Church, with which the **Roman Church** agrees, and not to the private ministry in cases of necessity, regarding which he has given his formal opinion above.

It is also of this public **Baptism** within the Church that the **Constitutions** <note>The *Apostolic Constitutions*, a 4th-century collection of ecclesiastical law once attributed to Clement of Rome.</note> attributed to **Clement** ⸸ speak, when they say that *it is not permitted to **Laypeople** to perform any priestly work, such as the SACRIFICE*, or **Baptism**; this is clear because it places it alongside *Sacrifice* <note>The Mass or Eucharist.</note>, which is a matter of the public office of the Church.

That this practice <note>The administration of sacraments by those other than priests.</note> was public in the Church during the purest centuries of **Christianity** appears from what **Scripture** says of **Philip**, <margin>*Acts 8:38*</margin> who baptized although he was only a **Deacon**; and **Ananias**, <margin>*Acts 9:18*</margin> who was only a **Layman**, baptized **St. Paul**. It appears from what three faithful witnesses have testified above. And finally by **Calvin**, who for his entire party had...

<margin>*</margin> *Tertullian, "On the Veiling of Virgins." It is not permitted for a woman to speak in the church: nor to teach, nor to dip <note>original: "tingere"; a synonym for baptizing</note>, nor to offer, etc.*

<margin>⸸</margin> *Canon 3, chapter 10. Neither is it permitted to a layman to perform any priestly work, such as Sacrifice, or Baptism.*

<meta>This page continues the author’s theological dispute with Protestant ministers regarding the nature of baptism. Having previously argued that lay baptism is a valid ancient practice, he now addresses the distinction between the baptism performed by John the Baptist and the baptism of Jesus Christ, using the Book of Acts to support his position.</meta>

<margin>Compare French [Confession of Faith] articles 28 and 35.</margin>
<margin>Acts 19:3, 4, 5.</margin>
One only needs to look back at what I say in my summary to see its clarity, which they try to obscure. They cannot deny what their *Confession of Faith* <note>Referring to the *Confessio Gallicana* (1559), the foundational statement of faith for French Protestants (Huguenots).</note> says: that the Baptism of Jesus Christ, once given, must not be repeated. If the Baptism of St. John had been the same as that of Jesus Christ, the Disciples at Ephesus mentioned in the 19th chapter of the *Acts* of the Apostles, who had been baptized *with John’s Baptism*, would not have been baptized *afterward in the Name of Jesus*, as verses 3 and 5 show.

What St. Paul says in verse 4 clearly shows the difference between these two Baptisms: For verse 3 having said that the Disciples *were baptized with John’s baptism*, St. Paul adds, *John indeed baptized with the baptism of repentance, telling the people that they should believe in him who was coming after him, that is, in Jesus who is the Christ*. After this it is said, *When they heard these things*—and what things? Doubtless those that St. Paul had just said in this 4th verse. *When they heard these things, they were baptized in the Name of Jesus*; does he not say there openly that after having heard the things St. Paul had just said, the Disciples who were baptized *with John’s Baptism* were *baptized in the Name of the Lord Jesus*?

It was not, then, by *John*, as these Gentlemen <note>The Protestant ministers the author is debating.</note> say—and consequently a long time before *hearing these things*—but immediately after *hearing these things* which the Apostle had just said; and this was done either by St. Paul or by someone who might have been with him, after which hands were laid upon them, and they received the Holy Spirit.

One only needs to follow the order of the words to see this truth, and to oppose it is to openly wage war against the very Text of the Spirit of God.

<meta>This page continues the author’s theological argument against Protestant "Ministers" (referred to as "these Gentlemen" on the previous page). <margin>John 1:33.</margin>
They bring forth in their own favor what actually works against them—namely, the words of St. John: *He who sent me to baptize with water said to me: He upon whom you see the Spirit descend and remain, this is he who baptizes with the Holy Spirit.* What do these words prove, except what no one disputes? Namely, that John’s baptism was from Heaven? And as St. Augustine says, *that the Father and the Son sent John?* What else do they prove if not the difference between the Baptism of John and that of Jesus Christ? Calling the former *Baptism of water*, and the latter *Baptism of the Holy Spirit?*

It is true that John’s baptism was *unto repentance and remission of sins*: but that does not prevent this contested difference, as both can be *unto repentance and remission of sins*, but in a different manner. Namely, John’s was to invite one to repentance and to believe in the Christ who was coming, as St. Paul speaks in verse 4 of the aforementioned chapter of the Acts of the Apostles, and *unto the remission of sins* by believing in the Christ who was coming to merit and acquire it by the shedding of his blood on the Cross—all of this to prepare the way for participation in the Lord’s benefits through baptism. In contrast, the baptism of Jesus Christ was given in repentance as a necessary disposition for its participation—and principally in repentance for the crucifixion of their Savior—and for the remission of sins acquired by the blood of the Cross and applied through baptism, as St.
<margin>Acts 2.</margin>
Peter teaches to the 3,000 Jews <note>Referring to the conversion of the 3,000 on the day of Pentecost (Acts 2:38-41).</note>.

It is in this way that the great St. Augustine reasoned:

---

> St. Augustine, *On Baptism, Against the Donatists*, Book 5, Chapter 10. <note>original: "S. Aug. l. 5. c. Don. de bapt. c. 10. Ut ab eo (Ioh. Bapt.) baptizatis in SPE remitterentur peccata, REIPSA verò in Domini Baptismo id fieret."</note> So that for those baptized by him (John the Baptist), sins were remitted in HOPE, but in ACTUAL FACT, this occurred in the Baptism of the Lord.

<summary>The author argues that while John the Baptist’s baptism was a divine preparation focused on hope and future repentance, only the Baptism of Jesus Christ provides the actual remission of sins through the Holy Spirit.</summary>

# Article XXV.

I have set forth in this article the Doctrine which the Catholic Church—taught by Jesus Christ our Lord and His blessed Apostles, and led by the Holy Spirit into all truth—has always held and shall hold until the end of the world, just as she has done until now, notwithstanding the opposition of all her Adversaries.

No matter how much the <term>Ministers of these Valleys</term> <note>Referring to the Protestant (Waldensian) pastors in the Alpine valleys of Piedmont, with whom the author is in theological conflict.</note> disguise it and represent it as they please, I would ask them to set aside the concern they pretend to have for me—that the Roman Church might not forgive my phrasing—for since I have the necessary approval in hand, they no longer need to maintain this fear. They feign this in a manner that shows that, in wanting to make their speeches witty, they only make them ridiculous to any judicious and reasonable person. They would perhaps prefer that I spoke like them, and used their expressions regarding this mystery, so that far from believing it, you would hold it in horror.

My expression has been approved as Catholic and <term>Orthodox</term>, <note>In this context, "orthodox" refers to "right-believing" according to Catholic tradition, rather than the Eastern Orthodox Church.</note> I hold to it, and I shall hold—

[I will hol]d as long as I live, no matter how much these Gentlemen turn it in as many directions as they wish—whether towards the <term>Lutherans</term>, the <term>Zwinglians</term>, the <term>Calvinists</term>, or the Roman Catholics, as they do: I shall always say of it, * "This is my Faith because it is the Catholic Faith." * <note>The author refers to the major branches of the Protestant Reformation: the followers of Martin Luther, Huldrych Zwingli, and John Calvin.</note>

This expression is that the Roman Church teaches that by the words of the Consecration which our Lord used in the institution of the Sacrament of the <term>Eucharistie</term> <note>Also known as Holy Communion or the Lord's Supper.</note>, saying, *This is my Body: This is my Blood*, the Bread is made His Body and the Wine His Blood, and that His Body and His Blood are in such a way in Heaven by their visible and natural presence that by the virtue of these words they acquire a sacramental presence in the Bread and Wine.

If the Roman Church did not teach this, it would not have been approved, as it has been with good reason, since these are the very terms of Session 13 * of the <term>Concile de Trente</term> <note>The Council of Trent (1545–1563) was the definitive ecumenical council of the Roman Catholic Church that responded to the Protestant Reformation.</note>.

If these Gentlemen approve of it, ask them why, instead of agreeing with it, they impugn and fight it through fifteen pages of writing? And from this, you will see their vanity revealed, for if they approve it, why do they not subscribe to it without entertaining you with fifteen pages of opposition?

The <term>transubstantiation</term> <note>The Catholic doctrine that the "substance" or essence of the bread and wine changes into the Body and Blood of Christ, while the "accidents" (appearance, taste) remain the same.</note> by which the substance of the Bread and of the Wine is converted into the substance of the

Body and the Blood of Jesus Christ by the words of the Consecration, does not fill me with horror, as these Gentlemen <note>Referring to the Protestant ministers mentioned on the previous page.</note> imagine, since I clearly state it when I say that by these words of the Consecration the Bread is made the Body of Christ, and the Wine His Blood: for a thing that is made into another is no longer what it was, and is the thing that it was not, through its change into it: everyone knows that being made a thing and <margin>To be made</margin> <note>original: *Fieri*. The Latin verb *fieri* serves as both "to be made" and "to become," which the author uses to argue for a change of substance.</note> becoming it is the same thing, which is why the Latin language expresses both in a single word.

Now, when Holy Scripture wants to say that Lot's wife was substantially changed into salt, it <margin>Gen. 19:26.</margin> says that she *became* or *was made a pillar of salt* \*. And when it wants to say that the waters of Egypt were substantially changed into blood, it says <margin>Exod. 7:19.</margin> that they *became Blood* \*: just as I would say that the water at the Wedding of Cana was made wine, when I wanted to say that it was changed into wine; meat is made flesh and wine blood, when both are converted into the flesh and blood of man, and are made his own substance.

<margin>Matth. 26.</margin> Jesus Christ says of the Bread he blessed, *This is my Body broken for you*: And of the Wine, <margin>John 6.</margin> *This is my Blood shed for you; my flesh is truly food and my blood truly drink. The bread that I shall give is my flesh; if you do not eat the flesh of the Son of Man you shall have no life in yourselves.*

Therefore, by the words of the Consecration, the Bread and the Wine are made the Body and the Blood of Jesus Christ,

Christ, as St. Augustine <note>A 4th-century bishop and one of the most influential theologians in Western Christianity.</note> said in the passage cited: *Before the Consecration they are Bread and Wine which nature formed: but after the Consecration they are the Flesh and the Blood which the Blessing consecrated.*

And in another place; *Before the words of Christ what is offered is Bread: after the words of Christ it is no longer called Bread, but Body.*

<margin>* St. Ambrose, book 4 *On the Sacraments*, ch. 4. <note>original: "Panis iste panis est..."</note> That bread is bread before the words of the Sacraments; when the consecration has occurred, from bread it becomes the Body of Christ.</margin>

<margin>* Tertullian, book 4 *Against Marcion*. <note>original: "Acceptum panem Corpus suum facit..."</note> Having taken the bread, he makes it his Body by saying "This is my Body."</margin>

St. Cyprian <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Carthage and a famous martyr.</note> shows that this is done by change when he says, *This bread changed not in appearance, but in NATURE is MADE flesh by the Omnipotence of the word.*

who baptizes is Christ: likewise it is he who by his Holy Spirit *MAKES* this his Flesh, & *CHANGES* it into Blood.

And here is determined the uncertainty in which these Gentlemen <note>The author’s Protestant or Jansenist theological opponents.</note> claim I held myself—based on their imagination that the dogma of <term>transubstantiation: the Catholic doctrine that the bread and wine at Mass literally become the body and blood of Christ while keeping their outward appearance.</term> fills me with horror. On the contrary, I believe it with the Church that teaches it, and as I believe it, I state it with these words: *The Bread & the Wine are MADE the Body & the Blood of Christ.*

Thus, I have no need for the precautions they trouble themselves to offer me. The thorny difficulties and the absurdities they claim follow from it are but chimeras <note>imaginary monsters or illusions; here, false arguments.</note> of their own minds. The Catholic Church long ago exposed the vanity of such ideas and has proven this dogma (which fills them with such horror) throughout every Age of Christianity, through its Doctors and its Councils, under pain of <term>anathema: a formal curse by a pope or council of the Church, excommunicating those who hold contrary beliefs.</term> for anyone who contradicts it. I shall now proceed to present some of these to you:

---

* <margin>St. Cyprian <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Carthage and early Christian martyr.</note>, *On the Supper of the Lord*.</margin> <note>original: "Panis iste non effigie sed natura mutatus omnipotentia verbi factus est Caro."</note> This bread, changed not in outward appearance but in nature, has been made Flesh by the omnipotence of the Word.
* <margin>St. Augustine, *On the Body of the Lord*.</margin> <note>original: "Sicut qui baptizat ipse Christus est : ita ipse est qui per Spiritum Sanctum hanc suam efficit Carnem & transfundit in Sanguinem."</note> Just as he who baptizes is Christ himself: so it is he himself who, through the Holy Spirit, makes this his Flesh and transforms it into Blood.
* <margin>St. Cyril <note>Cyril of Jerusalem, a 4th-century theologian known for his "Catechetical Lectures" for new converts.</note>, *Catechesis 4*.</margin> <note>original: "Hoc sciens & pro certissimo habens panem hunc qui videtur à nobis non esse panem, etiamsi gustus sentiat esse panem, sed esse Corpus Christi..."</note> Knowing this and holding it as most certain: that this bread which we see is not bread, even if the taste perceives it to be bread, but is the Body of Christ; and the wine which is beheld by us, although it seems to the sense of taste to be wine, is nevertheless not wine: but is the Blood of Christ.
* <margin>Gregory of Nyssa <note>A 4th-century theologian and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers" who shaped early Christian doctrine.</note>, *Catechetical Oration*, ch. 37.</margin> <note>original: "Rectè nunc quoque credo Dei verbo sanctificatum panem in Dei verbi Corpus transmutari."</note> Rightly now I also believe that bread sanctified by the word of God is transmuted into the Body of the Word of God.

who baptizes is Christ: likewise it is he who, by his Holy Spirit, MAKES this his Flesh, and CHANGES it into Blood.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And thus the uncertainty in which these Gentlemen <note>The author is likely referring to Protestant critics or "the Gentlemen of Port-Royal" or similar theological opponents.</note> claim I remained is resolved, based on their imagination that the dogma of transubstantiation <term>transubstantiation: the Catholic doctrine that the substance of bread and wine is changed into the actual Body and Blood of Christ during Mass, leaving only the outward appearances.</term> fills me with horror; whereas, in truth, I believe it alongside the Church that teaches it. And as I believe it, I declare it with these words: * *The Bread and the Wine are MADE the Body and Blood of Christ.*

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Thus, I have no need for the precautions with which they try to "save" me the trouble. The thorny difficulties and absurdities they claim follow from this doctrine are merely chimeras of their own minds. The Catholic Church has long since exposed the vanity of such ideas, proving this dogma (which causes them such horror) through every century of Christianity, and by its Doctors and Councils, under pain of anathema <note>A formal curse or excommunication by the Church.</note> against anyone who contradicts it. I am going to present some of these to you:

---

\* St. Cyprian, *On the Lord's Supper*: This bread, changed not in outward appearance but in nature, is made Flesh by the omnipotence of the Word. <note>original Latin: "Panis iste non effigie sed natura mutatus omnipotentia verbi factus est Caro."</note>

\* St. Augustine, *Book on the Body of the Lord*: Just as it is Christ himself who baptizes: so it is he himself who, through the Holy Spirit, makes this his Flesh and transforms it into Blood. <note>original Latin: "Sicut qui baptizat ipse Christus est : ita ipse est qui per Spiritum Sanctum hanc suam efficit Carnem & transfundit in Sanguinem."</note>

\* St. Cyril, *Catechesis 4*: Knowing this and holding it as most certain, that this bread which is seen by us is not bread, even if the taste feels it to be bread, but is the Body of Christ; and the wine which is beheld by us, although to the sense of taste it seems to be wine, is yet not wine: but is the Blood of Christ. <note>original Latin: "Hoc sciens & pro certissimo habens panem hunc qui videtur à nobis non esse panem, etiamsi gustus sentiat esse panem, sed esse Corpus Christi..."</note>

\* Gregory of Nyssa, *Catechetical Oration*, ch. 37: Rightly now do I also believe that the bread, sanctified by the word of God, is transmuted into the Body of the Word of God. <note>original Latin: "Rectè nunc quoque credo Dei verbo sanctificatum panem in Dei verbi Corpus transmutari."</note>

<summary>The author explicitly affirms his belief in the doctrine of Transubstantiation and provides a series of Latin citations from Church Fathers to prove that the bread and wine are truly changed into Christ's body and blood.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s presentation of historical authorities supporting the doctrine of the Real Presence, transitioning from Greek theologians like John Damascene to prominent Latin Fathers and medieval scholastics like St. Ambrose and Peter Lombard.</meta>

The holy and ancient Doctor **St. Ambrose** <note>Ambrose (c. 339–397 AD) was the Bishop of Milan and one of the four original Doctors of the Church; he was instrumental in the conversion of St. Augustine.</note>, **Bishop of Milan**, speaks in this way: *“The word of Christ, which was able to make out of nothing that which was not, can it not CHANGE the things that are into what they were not? For it is no less a thing to give new natures to things than to CHANGE them.”*

**Lombard** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160), a professor and Bishop of Paris, whose "Sentences" became the standard textbook for theology students throughout the Middle Ages.</note>, whom these Gentlemen have cited against me, settles the matter clearly: *“the SUBSTANCE of the Bread and the Wine is not there, even though the <term>APPEARANCES: Known in theology as "species," these are the sensory qualities of bread and wine (like taste, smell, and texture) that remain even after the substance has changed.</term> remain.”*

How would it be possible for me to hold in horror a dogma so clearly proposed and taught by such holy and learned Doctors? In good faith, do your Gentlemen presume to know more, and to be more holy than these sacred instruments of the Spirit of God, and to understand the Scriptures better than they? If they hold this doctrine in horror, it is because the truth is hated and abhorred by its enemies.

<margin>Art. 36.</margin> <note>This likely refers to Article 36 of the Gallican Confession (1559), the foundational statement of faith for French Protestants (Huguenots).</note>

* St. Ambrose, *On Those Being Initiated*, chapter 9. <note>original: "Sermo ergo Christi qui potuit ex nihilo facere quod non erat, non potest ea quæ sunt in id mutare quod non erat ? non enim minus est nouas rebus dare quàm mutare naturas." — The word of Christ, therefore, which was able to make out of nothing that which was not, is it not able to change those things which are into that which they were not? For it is no less a work to give new qualities to things than to change their natures.</note>

* Lombard, book 4, distinction 11. <note>original: "Post Consecrationem substantia Panis & Vini ibi non est quamuis species maneant." — After the Consecration, the substance of the Bread and Wine is not there, although the appearances remain.</note>

<margin>John 20:22</margin>

<meta>This page continues a theological defense of the Catholic doctrine of the Real Presence in the Eucharist. The author is responding to Protestant critics (the "Gentlemen") by interpreting a specific passage from Saint Augustine regarding how Christ "carried himself in his own hands" during the Last Supper.</meta>

...the whole [passage] <note>The OCR "ge" likely completes the word "passage" from the previous page.</note> is that it is impossible for a man to carry [himself] upon his own hands, and to do this is fitting for Christ alone: upon which I reasoned in this way: If Jesus Christ had understood that the bread was his Body in sign and figure only, the saying of this faithful Witness <note>Refers to Saint Augustine as a reliable witness to Christian tradition.</note> would not be true, that it is impossible for a man to carry [himself] upon his hands, for it is quite easy for him to carry his own image and figure there.

They keep silent about these words of **St. Augustine**, and my reasoning upon them, because all of that causes them trouble; they judge it better to remain silent, doing the same with most of the proofs I bring, because they imagine that you will not take the trouble to compare their writing with mine to see if they respond or not. By only alleging what they want from my proofs, and twisting them as they please, they can say, as they do, that they find it easy to answer them.

However, it is clear that when Our Lord carried his Body, he carried there his true **Sacramental Body**, present there in a **real manner**, but a sacramental one, as the **Council of Trent** <note>The 16th-century council that clarified Catholic doctrine in response to the Reformation.</note> says, which I have cited above. And it is in this manner that the same Holy Father <note>Saint Augustine.</note>, in the passage that these Gentlemen object to me, saying that he carried himself *in a certain way* <note>original Latin: *quodammodo*</note> in his hands, [speaks] to exclude not the truth of the Body of Christ, nor the real and Sacramental manner of his presence, but to exclude the natural manner—which I grant to them.

C <note>This "C" is a catchword, a printer's tool indicating the first word of the next page to ensure the sheets were bound in the correct order.</note>

<margin>**St. Augustine** on the title of **Psalm 33**: Carrying oneself in one's hands is impossible for a man, and can only apply to Christ alone. <note>original Latin: "S. Aug. in tit. ps. 33. Portare se in manibus homini impossibile est, soliq; Christo convenire potest."</note></margin>

<summary>The author argues that Saint Augustine's description of Christ carrying "himself" proves the Real Presence, as carrying a mere symbol would not be "impossible" for a normal man. He distinguishes between a "natural" physical presence and the "sacramental" real presence defined by the Council of Trent.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's refutation of Protestant arguments regarding the Real Presence. It specifically addresses how Christ carried his own body at the Last Supper and defends the authenticity of Patristic citations from Saint Augustine and Saint John Chrysostom.</meta>

What he took in his hands, and what the faithful know, is the bread of which the Evangelists <note>The authors of the Gospels: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note> say that he took, blessed, and broke; but what he carried in his hands after the words: *This is my Body*, *This is my Blood*, was in some way—namely, the sacramental way mentioned above—*his Body and his Blood*, as he had said to his disciples.

They respond with the same ease to the other passage from **St. Augustine** that I had cited, which says that *before the words of consecration, there is the bread and wine that nature formed: but after them, there is the flesh and Blood of Christ that the blessing has consecrated.* To this, they have no other escape <note>original: "défaite" — an excuse or loophole.</note> than to say that this passage is spurious <note>original: "supposé" — a claim that a text is forged or falsely attributed to an author.</note>, imagining that you are credulous enough to believe it without them taking the trouble to prove it to you.

They respond with the same ease again to the passage from **St. Chrysostom** <note>Saint John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), a prominent Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquence.</note>, not taking the trouble to report it to you as I cited it, but bringing you their gloss <note>original: "glose" — an explanatory note or interpretation often added to a text.</note> and interpretation. They also imagine that you will rely enough on their explanation without bringing you the text to make a comparison, and see if what this *Excellent Servant of God* pronounces is the same thing as what they tell you. Compare the text with their explanation, and you will see quite a difference there.

What they object regarding this same Father concerning baptism is not of the same strength—far from being of a greater one, as they say—for first, they do not report his actual words, but their own tradition, to which I am not obliged to give credit. After that, the institution of the **Sacrament of the Eucharist** is quite different from baptism, for there are no words of consecration over the water here, as there are over the Bread and the Wine.

<summary>The author argues that the bread becomes Christ's Body specifically after the words of consecration and accuses his opponents of dismissing Saint Augustine’s writings as forgeries. He further distinguishes the unique nature of the Eucharist's consecration from the rite of baptism.</summary>
<keywords>Eucharist, Transubstantiation, Saint Augustine, Saint John Chrysostom, consecration, baptism, Real Presence, Patristics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the Real Presence in the Eucharist, specifically addressing a passage from Saint John Chrysostom that opponents (likely Protestant theologians) have attempted to recontextualize by comparing the Eucharist to the Mystery of Baptism.</meta>

q

In short, it proves nothing against the present subject, for it only means to say that the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit work everything at baptism, and that the Angels who are spectators there could not describe the manner of this ineffable work. What does that do, I ask you, to weaken the force of what he had said: that at the Sacrament of the Eucharist <note>The Holy Communion or Lord's Supper.</note> *one must obey God, and not contradict Him at all, even though everything that is said seems contrary to our thoughts and our eyes, inasmuch as His word, "This is my Body, This is my Blood," is infallible, and our sense is easily deceived?*

What more can I say, except that this very passage they object to me confounds them: for if at baptism we must not believe our senses, which see only water and hear only words, but believe the word of God which teaches that the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit work everything at baptism; so also in the Eucharist, we must not believe our senses to believe only the Bread and the Wine which they see, taste, and touch: but the word of Jesus Christ who says *This is my Body, This is my Blood.* And this is the just conclusion that I draw against them from the very propositions they had brought against me.

Even though there is nothing more futile than the evasions of these Gentlemen <note>A polite but firm reference to his Protestant opponents.</note> regarding the proofs I bring, nevertheless, believing they have satisfied them well, they try to divert me <note>original: "me donnent le change" — a hunting metaphor meaning to throw someone off the scent or change the subject.</note> to see if I will respond with as much ease to their arguments as they did to mine.

> Saint Chrysostom, Homilies 60 and 61 to the people of Antioch. <note>original: "S. Chrys. hom. 60. 61. ad pop. Antioch. It is a constant truth that if the Sacraments did not have some resemblance to the things of which they are Sacraments, they would not be Sacraments at all. And for that reason, Jesus Christ instituted the Bread and the Wine, having a resemblance to the Body and Blood of Jesus Christ, of which they are the Sacraments, the signs, the appearances <term>species: the outward properties (like taste, color, or smell) that remain even after the underlying substance has changed</term>, and the forms. These being for the Soul to nourish and strengthen it, just as the others do for the body.

This is also why they are called by the name of what they signify and contain, namely the Body and the Blood of the Lord; the certain manner in which He says this is done is the mystical and sacramental manner of which we spoke above—just as the Sacrament of Faith, namely Baptism, is called *Faith*, not because faith is absent from it, but present in children through the Sacrament, and in adults by their own act.

They cite Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–457 AD), an influential bishop whose writings were frequently used by both sides in the Reformation to argue about the nature of the Eucharist.</note> not in his own words, but in the version they have made of them, which I could reject as suspicious; but to give them every possible advantage, let us suppose he said that *Jesus Christ honored the visible symbols with the Name of His Body and His Blood, not by changing their nature, but by adding grace to nature, and that they remain in their own substance*.

Next, I say that just as by these visible signs he understands the *sacramental species* <term>species: the outward, sensible qualities of the bread and wine (like appearance and taste) that remain after consecration</term>, so too by their *nature and substance* are meant their *accidents* <term>accidents: in Aristotelian philosophy, these are qualities (like color, weight, or smell) that exist in a substance but are not the substance itself</term>—namely *color*, *flavor*, and *form*. He does not mean *substance* in the sense that it is distinguished from accidents, which Aristotle places in the first Category <note>In Aristotle's *Categories*, "Substance" is the primary way of being, while "accidents" like quality or quantity are secondary.</note>. Rather, he speaks of the *essence and nature* of the accidents—that is, the *nature* and *essence* that have remained there with their other natural properties, which some Philosophers call the *Thing itself*.

That this Doctor intended nothing else by these words is apparent from what he says in another place, where he states that Jesus Christ did not want us to dwell on the *nature of the things that are seen*, but rather that we should believe in the **CHANGE** that is accomplished here by grace. <note>The author is quoting Theodoret’s *Dialogues*, Book 1. Original Latin: "Ut hic non attenderetur ad naturam eorum quæ videntur, sed ut crederemus mutationi quæ hic fit ex gratia."</note>

...their objections mentioned above. And así, you see the ease with which I overturn their strongest arguments <note>original: "machines" - a 17th-century term for complex logical structures or rhetorical devices.</note>.

There is no doubt that the Body of Christ is the nourishment of the Soul primarily and of faith: but since the Soul and the Body make up but one person, both the one and the other will be participants in the remission of sins acquired by the death of Christ, just as both have committed and continue to commit them. And both, through and after this remission, will be raised to Heaven by the merit of the death of this Savior: thus both participate in this benefit, even though it is primarily the Soul which is the seat of faith and produces its acts, according to these words:

> *He who comes to me shall not hunger, and he who believes in me shall never thirst.*

This is why this eating is principally spiritual, which helps and saves without the bodily eating, whereas the latter without the former not only serves for nothing but is harmful: when Our Lord says to his Disciples:
> *Take, eat, this is my Body*,

# Article XXVI.

<margin>Daniel 8:11</margin>
As there is nothing in the Roman Church that the [Protestant] Ministers persuade their people to loathe more than the Mass—abolishing this *continual sacrifice* <note>In Catholic theology, the "continual sacrifice" refers to the daily celebration of the Eucharist; the author references Daniel 8:11, where the "daily sacrifice" is taken away by a prideful power, a passage often associated in polemics with the Antichrist.</note> among themselves, just as Daniel predicted the Antichrist would do—I have also chosen, in the summary I presented to you, to make it a separate Article. And though it is only a page and a half long, these Gentlemen <note>The Protestant ministers.</note> tell you that I have used all my efforts there to prove its validity.

You maintain brotherhood with the Lutherans, who state in their *Augsburg Confession* * that *they are falsely accused of having abolished the MASS, since they have RETAINED it among them, and celebrate it with great reverence, in Latin, and with almost all the customary ceremonies*. Your own Confession of Faith from the year 1655 subscribes to that Confession.

<margin>Art. 33.</margin>
Despite this *Mass* that the Lutherans have retained, you do not cease to maintain brotherhood and form one single Church Body with them. Yet, for this same *Mass*, you refuse to live in Communion with the Roman Church.

Your Ministers subscribe to your Confession of 1655; this [Confession] in turn subscribes to the Mass retained by the Augsburg Confession. And yet, they call this retained Mass a *so-called sacrifice* and *an illusion* for which PRINCIPALLY they cannot have any *Communion* with

* <note>original: "Falsò accusantur Ecclesiæ nostræ quòd Missam aboleant, retinetur enim Missa apud nos, & summâ reuerentiâ celebratur, &c."</note> Chapter 20. Our churches are falsely accused of abolishing the Mass; for the Mass is retained among us and is celebrated with the highest reverence, etc.

<summary>The author highlights a perceived hypocrisy in the Protestant movement: the French Reformed Church maintains fellowship with Lutherans, who officially retain the Mass, while simultaneously rejecting the Catholic Church primarily because of the Mass.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the inconsistencies of Protestant "Ministers." *...with the Roman Church.* What contradictions?

For this reason, I am expanding more at length on this Article than on the previous ones, so that you may see if this *Mass*—which you retain among the *Lutherans* and detest among the *Catholics*—is as "horrible" as they have made it out to be to you. I am going to follow these Gentlemen <note>The Protestant ministers.</note> step-by-step to consider the proofs they put forward.

To start, I must inform you that the *Roman Church* holds that the sacrifice of the Cross is the only absolute sacrifice, to which all others (both those preceding and following it) relate, as images relate to their archetype, and copies to their original. It is the only sacrifice that has merited our entire redemption, and it alone is the perfect price for it.

The question, therefore, is to know if—just as there were various sacrifices under the <term>Law</term> <note>The Old Testament/Mosaic Law.</note> to prefigure the Sacrifice of the Cross to come—there ought also to be some sacrifice under the <term>Gospel</term> <note>The New Testament era.</note> to celebrate the memory of that of the Cross, and to apply its benefit to believers? The *Catholic Church* maintains that there is, and your *Ministers* deny it; we must see who is right.

Instead of first responding to the proofs I had brought forward and demonstrating their invalidity (if they were able), they instead heap up a large number of passages to prove through them the contrary of what those proofs showed you.

Recall the warning I gave you previously regarding the difference between the absolute and principal Sacrifice of the Cross—to which all others relate as to their original and model—and you will see in a few words the dissipation of everything they oppose on this subject.

When, therefore, the Catholic Church posits a <term>propitiatory sacrifice</term> <note>A sacrifice intended to reconcile humanity with God and obtain the forgiveness of sins.</note>, it is not to merit our redemption in the first place, as if that one [on the Cross] had not merited it fully and perfectly; rather, it is to remind us of it and to apply its merit for our salvation. For just as the unique sacrifice of the Cross does not prevent there having been several sacrifices in the <term>Ancient Law</term> <note>The Mosaic Law or Old Testament.</note> that prefigured it and applied its power, and as those sacrifices served to remit sins—which is why they were...

<summary>The author clarifies that the Catholic Mass does not claim to repeat or replace Christ’s one-time sacrifice on the Cross, but rather functions to apply the merits of that singular event to the lives of believers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's theological defense of the Catholic Mass, specifically addressing interpretations of the Epistle to the Hebrews. The previous page concluded by noting that Saint Paul’s descriptions of Christ's sacrifice refer specifically to his "bloody oblation."</meta>

Christ, who was prefigured by the sacrifices of the Law <note>The religious rituals and animal sacrifices prescribed in the Old Testament.</note> and fulfilled in his death, as appears by the contrast he [Paul] makes between the sacrifices of the Law, which were offered with the blood of others, and that which he offered himself, when he entered once and for all into the Holy of Holies by his own blood, having obtained for us an eternal redemption.

He speaks of the bloody <term>oblation</term> <note>A formal ritual offering to God; here referring specifically to the crucifixion.</note> which was performed to acquire for us the full and perfect remission of our sins, and our perfect sanctification.

<margin>
Hebrews 9:28.
Hebrews 10:10.
</margin>
*He offered himself once to take away the sins of many; and by the offering made once of the Body of Jesus Christ, we are sanctified.*

<margin>
Hebrews 9:26.
Hebrews 10:18.
</margin>
And that these passages exclude only any bloody offering after that of the Cross is clearly evident from what is said: *Otherwise it would be necessary for him to suffer many times since the beginning of the world.*

Thus, it is true that where there is remission of sins (that is to say, perfectly acquired and merited), there is no offering—namely, a bloody one, which involves the death of the thing offered for sin—to acquire pardon, as if it were not already fully acquired.

Here, then, is established the perfection of the merit of Christ’s death. And just as there is a single offering, there is also a perfect sanctification and eternal salvation for those for whom it is made.

Does all this prevent a commemorative, representative, and applicative sacrifice <note>A sacrifice that remembers, portrays, and applies the spiritual benefits of a past event to the present.</note> of the fruits of his perfect merit? Certainly not: for if it did, Holy Scripture and the Church, which is its true interpreter, would not propose to us one of this nature to be offered by Christians until the end of the world, in order to commemorate, represent, and apply such a salutary sacrifice.

<summary>The author argues that while Christ's sacrifice on the Cross was a unique, "bloody" event that perfectly achieved human redemption, it does not preclude the existence of a "commemorative" sacrifice (the Mass) to apply those merits to believers.</summary>

St. Ambrose looks to this when he says: *When we SACRIFICE, Christ is present, Christ is IMMOLATED: For our Passover sacrificed is CHRIST.*

[Melchi]zedek <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of Melchizedek, the mysterious priest-king from Genesis.</note>—that of the Passover—and commanded his Apostles, and in their person all their successors, to do this in memory of him, and to announce by this means his death until the end of the World.

Jesus Christ is this eternal priest, and his <term>priesthood</term> <note>Original: "sacrificature."</note> is eternal; his sacrifice is eternal also and continual, as we have learned above from Daniel. He does not remain always in the world, but goes to the right hand of God his Father. What other sacrifice can one say that Jesus Christ continues always to make through his Vicars and Ministers, similar to that of Melchizedek—which was a sacrifice of bread and wine—if not the sacrifice of the Eucharist?

This is so clear that there is cause for wonder that there are people who dare to contradict it, and oppose the common and universal sentiment of all orthodox Christendom for nearly 17 centuries, with the weight of which I am going to charge the weakness of their imaginations.

If St. Paul does not speak of this sacrifice of bread and wine of Melchizedek, nor of the relationship it has with the Eucharist, it is because it was a truth so certain—especially to those to whom he wrote—that he did not judge it necessary to speak to them of it expressly. First, one which is God, to whom it is offered to honor him, to appease him, to ask him for the goods of which one has need, and to give him thanks for those one has received. The other object is man, to whom it is offered, not as an act of homage as to God, but so that he may receive it and enjoy its benefit.

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of the Catholic doctrine of the Eucharist against Protestant critics. # Apostles.

If the Gentlemen Ministers <note>The author refers to the Protestant pastors of the Waldensian "Valleys" of Piedmont, with whom he is in theological conflict.</note> of these Valleys have the courage to hurl at me, as they do, all these fine insults, I leave to them the honor and the reward they deserve for it before God and before men.

However, consider for a moment whether their conclusions are of any different nature than those they attribute to the entire Church, to all the aforementioned Ancients <note>The "Ancients" refers to the Church Fathers—the early influential theologians of Christianity.</note>, and to me.

The question is whether the Sacrament of the Eucharist is a visible and propitiatory <term>propitiatory: An offering intended to appease God's justice and obtain the forgiveness of sins.</term> Sacrifice?

They do not know where to find a passage in St. Augustine for me that—in exchange for the one I brought forward—testifies to the contrary; and finding none, they go and bring me one that formally affirms what they deny: * *The visible Sacrifice is a Sacrament, that is to say, a sacred sign of the invisible Sacrifice, and what men call a Sacrifice is a sign of the true Sacrifice.* <note>original: "Sacrificium visibile inuisibilis Sacrificij Sacramentum, idest sacrum signum est... Illud quod ab hominibus appellatur Sacrificium signũ est veri Sacrificij."</note>

If I had leafed through all of St. Augustine, could I ever have found more express proofs of this truth than these?

He says that the Eucharist is a visible Sacrifice, and a Sacrament or *sacred sign of the true and invisible Sacrifice*. And what more could I want? *The sign bears the name of the thing signified*, they say; *The thing signified is the true, invisible, and propitiatory Sacrifice of the Cross. And the sacred sign is the visible Sacrifice*. Therefore, this visible Sacrifice is a propitiatory Sacrifice. What more do I ask? See, Gentlemen, how incontestable this truth is.

->R<-

***

\* St. Augustine, *The City of God*, Book 10, Chapter 5. *The visible sacrifice is the Sacrament of the invisible Sacrifice, that is, it is a sacred sign, as below. That which is called Sacrifice by men is a sign of the true Sacrifice.*

<summary>The author uses a quote from St. Augustine, ironically provided by his opponents, to argue that the Eucharist is a visible and propitiatory sacrifice. <margin>Malachi 1:10, 11.</margin>
> There, God says to the Jews: *I have no pleasure in you, says the Lord of hosts, and I will not accept an offering from your hands: but from the rising of the sun even to the going down of the sun, my Name shall be great among the nations, and in EVERY PLACE incense shall be offered to my Name, and a PURE OFFERING, for my Name shall be great among the nations, says the Lord of hosts.*

St. Augustine has just said that this "pure offering" is the Sacrifice of the Christians offered in every place and according to the order of Melchizedek <note>The "order of Melchizedek" refers to the mysterious priest-king in Genesis who offered bread and wine; Catholics view this as a precursor to the Eucharist.</note>; in countless places throughout his works, he calls that Sacrifice the Sacrifice of the Mass. I am now going to show what he does.

It is evident that in verse 10, God is not speaking of the sacrifices "improperly so-called" of the Jews—such as prayer, repentance, alms, praise, thanksgiving, and generally the good works of the faithful Israelites—but he is speaking of sacrifices "properly so-called," which were external and sensible <term>sensible: in this context, it means "perceptible to the senses," referring to physical, tangible offerings like bread or animals rather than abstract concepts like thoughts.</term> offerings made according to the ordinances of the ceremonial law.

This appears clearly from what precedes: for he speaks of Gates and an Altar, saying at the beginning of this 10th verse: *Who is there among you who would shut the doors, and kindle the fire on my Altar for nothing?* This clearly shows that he is speaking of sacrifices...

Just as by the offerings of the Jews, of which God says that he will not accept them, the preaching of the Prophets is not intended, but rather the external Sacrifices of the Ceremonial Law; likewise, by this pure Offering, it is clear that one must not understand it as the preaching of the Gospel or the knowledge of God, seeing as that was already sufficiently expressed by the preceding words: *My Name shall be great among the nations*. This can only come about through the knowledge of this Name, and knowledge can only come through teaching, which is that of the Gospel. Therefore, it must be understood as an external and sensible <note>Meaning "perceptible to the senses" or physical, as opposed to purely spiritual.</note> Offering, which can be none other than that of the Eucharist.

Furthermore, it is ridiculous to understand by this pure offering the persons of the faithful themselves.

1. Because it will not be all kinds of faithful who will offer this pure offering, but only persons whom God will have sanctified for that purpose; just as in the offerings of the Jews, those who lit the fire on the altar were not all the Israelites, but specifically the priests and Levites. <margin>Malachi 3:3.</margin>

the offering, saying: *A pure offering shall be offered to my name;*
Now, as the cause is not the effect, nor the agent their action, nor the reverse: likewise the offering is not the offerers, nor are the offerers the offering.

I add to this that one cannot understand by this pure offering spiritual service, nor religious worship consisting of praise, prayers, alms, and other good works, because that is a worship for all times, as much under the Law as under the Gospel; but one was as much obliged to these duties under the former as under the latter. Therefore, this pure Offering, being foretold by God to be offered to his name under the Gospel—and not having been so previously—must necessarily be understood as a new offering particular to this <term>Economy of grace</term> <note>In theology, an "Economy" refers to God's specific plan or management of the world during a particular era of salvation history.</note>, which can be understood with no greater reason than as that of the Eucharist. This is very well represented by the continual and daily sacrifice; namely, this most holy offering among all the offerings made by the fire of the love of God, of zeal for his glory, and of charity for one's neighbor.

<margin>Isaiah 64:6.</margin>
I add further to this that if our good works are like "filthy rags" <note>original: "drap ſoüillé de la vierge." This is a reference to Isaiah 64:6; in the Latin Vulgate and older French translations, human righteousness is compared to "panni menstruatae" (the rags of a menstruating woman) to emphasize human imperfection before God.</note>, what pure offering is offered in the name of God other than that one?

And so that I say nothing offhand without proof acceptable to both Catholics and their adversaries, it is right that I accompany myself with the approval of the most shining lights of the Christian Church of the first centuries.

I will bring forward only two: one shall be St. Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), the Archbishop of Constantinople known for his eloquent preaching and his liturgy, which is still used in Eastern Christianity.</note> for the Churches of the East, and the other St. Augustine <note>Augustine of Hippo (354–430), a North African bishop whose writings profoundly shaped Western Christian theology.</note> for the Churches of the West.

you will see it all the more clearly as you compare it with the <term>figures</term> <note>In this context, "figures" refers to "types" or "shadows"—Old Testament events or persons that prefigure New Testament realities.</note> and promises that God had given regarding it under the Old Testament.

The fulfillment is found in the words of Our Lord to his twelve Apostles, *do this in memory of me*; He offered his Body and his blood in sacrifice <margin>Hebrews 10:5, 6, 7</margin> to God for the redemption of the human race. This Body of which he had said, *You wanted neither offering nor sacrifice: But you have <term>appropriated</term> <note>original: "approprié." In the cited passage of Hebrews, this refers to God "preparing" or "fitting" a human body for Christ to inhabit and sacrifice.</note> a Body for me, here I am, O God, to do your will*, and what he offered to God he presented to his Apostles, and commanded them to do what he was doing.

Listen on this matter to three holy <term>Doctors</term> <note>A "Doctor of the Church" is a saint recognized for making a significant contribution to theology or doctrine.</note> who knew incomparably more than all your <term>Ministers</term> <note>The author is addressing a Protestant audience, referring to their clergy.</note>:

St. Cyprian, Bishop of Carthage, living in the year 250, says: *He first offered himself in sacrifice to the Father, and commanded the SAME to be done in memory of him*.

The excellent servant of God, St. Chrysostom, speaks in the same way: *He commanded that HE BE OFFERED in the place of victims and sheep*.

St. Augustine shall be the third witness of this truth: *Jesus Christ, from his Body and his blood, instituted a SACRIFICE according to the order of Melchizedek*.

To this word the Catholic Church has conformed for nearly 17 centuries; She has always believed and practiced what she teaches and commands above; the Church of the golden ages <note>Referring to the early centuries of the Christian Church, often viewed as a period of purity and apostolic zeal.</note> was the first to enter this race, her faithful Witnesses have attested it to us, her Councils assure us of it, and the Church of the following centuries even unto us has followed in her footsteps; the unity of the Spirit which leads her, of the Head who governs her successively since St.
<margin>Ephesians 4:4-5.</margin>
Peter, shows us her Faith always *one*, *one* her Hope, and *one* her Communion.

Read and reread well, Gentlemen, this sacred word; it is the Arsenal which provides weapons to the

Article 26.

...destruction of your errors; it is she who will make you wise unto salvation, if you listen to her within herself, and follow the understanding, not of private men like your Innovators <note>original: "Nouateurs"; a polemical term used by Catholic writers to characterize Protestant reformers as people introducing dangerous novelties rather than restoring ancient truths.</note>, but of the Spirit of truth, who continually dictates it to his Church, according to the promise of Jesus Christ.

Be astonished, Gentlemen <note>original: "Messieurs"; the author is addressing the Protestant leadership or ministers directly.</note>, that it is preached to you on one hand, along with your Confession of 1655 <note>The 1655 revision of the Gallican Confession, the foundational statement of faith for French Reformed (Huguenot) churches.</note>, that the Church cannot fail or be reduced to nothing, and yet you wish to continue believing what you are told—that she has fallen into ruin and desolation—so that you may remain separated from her on this false pretext, and that there is no true Religion in the world but yours, even though it was born into the world only a century and a half ago.

Be astonished further that a few deserters of their profession, and of the Religion of the entire Universe and of all the Ages of Christianity, have presumed to be the only ones to rightly understand the Word of the Lord; and that there are men so carried away by passion that they believe nothing to be in conformity with that word except their own thoughts, and wish only to employ their talents to preach to you, in the twilight of Christianity, perseverance in the desertion of the Truth and of the Church of Jesus Christ, in order to keep you under the yoke of their imaginations.

<margin>
Genesis 6.
1 Timothy 3:15.
</margin>
As for me, I praise God that he has awakened my conscience to draw me out in his time, and lead me by his hand into the bosom of the Holy Spouse of his beloved Son, who is truly that Holy Catholic Church which I believe in according to the Apostles’ Creed <note>original: "Simbole des Apôtres".</note>. It is there that I wish to live and die, because outside of this *Ark* <note>A traditional reference to Noah's Ark; just as the Ark was the only refuge from the Great Flood (Genesis 6), the author argues the Church is the only refuge from spiritual destruction.</note> there is no salvation, and outside of this *House* <note>referencing 1 Timothy 3:15, where the Church is called the "house of God" and the "pillar and foundation of truth."</note> no heritage.

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s polemical defense against the Protestant ministers ("Messieurs"). It follows from the previous page's discussion of the "desertion of the True Church," shifting now to address the personal insults the author has faced and retorting with a counter-accusation of heresy and schism.</meta>

Let these Gentlemen <note>original: "ces Messieurs"; a recurring reference to the Protestant ministers or leadership the author is debating.</note> conceive as much as they wish the hope of the contrary of such a salutary duty, and for this purpose do everything that seems right to them in speech and in writing; it is they who will be left with the burden of giving an account of their souls and of yours before Him who has forbidden anyone to separate from His Church, and it is you who must give an account of your perseverance in this separation, notwithstanding His calling to the contrary.

Let these Gentlemen play the Apostles as much as they please, to associate me with the enemies of Christ and of His Apostles: Judas, Demas, Hymenaeus, Philetus, Alexander, and others, and let them say all the most bitter things that their animosity has been able to exhale against me.

<margin>Matthew 11:18. <note>In this verse, Jesus notes that people said John the Baptist "has a demon."</note></margin>
<margin>Acts 26:24. <note>The Roman governor Festus tells Saint Paul, "You are out of your mind!"</note></margin>
I am not surprised by it; for the Jews also said of Saint John the Baptist that *he had a demon*, and of Saint Paul that *he was out of his mind*, because they exhorted them to believe in the Truth; and the heretic Ebion <note>The Ebionites were an early Jewish Christian sect; the author uses this historical reference to show that the "true" faith has always been attacked by those who left it.</note> called this Holy Apostle *an Apostate of the Law*, because he had embraced the Faith of Christ and had joined His Church.

<margin>Revelation 9. <note>Reference to the "star fallen from heaven" and the smoke from the bottomless pit that darkened the sun.</note></margin>
I could retort against them and their Innovators <note>original: "Novateurs"; a common Catholic term for the leaders of the Reformation, implying they invented new doctrines rather than following tradition.</note> all their comparisons, and say: 1. That it is they who follow the *Stars fallen from Heaven* who, in the preceding century, have—with the smoke of their opinions—obscured the sun and the air of all that was clearest in the Church, and divided Christendom into so many lamentable schisms of Lutherans, Calvinists, Zwinglians, and others who are seen in the world today.

2. That it is they who follow the *Apostates* who, deserting the Church, have betrayed her Spouse and her Lord, as Judas did; and as Demas, Hymenaeus, Philetus, and Alexander <note>Biblical figures mentioned in the Epistles of Paul as men who abandoned the faith or spoke against it.</note> have loved the present world, and have left their Religion and profession

<meta>This page continues the author’s sharp rebuttal against Protestant accusations of apostasy. ...sion to create separate Sects, and to divide the unity of the Church.

3. That it is they who follow these deserters of the truth, and wallow in the heavy fall into which the apostasy of their vow and of the Church of the whole world has unfortunately plunged them.

4. That since they are not Apostles, nor like the Apostles, nor is the Reformation they preach to you an orthodox and Apostolic Religion [for <note>The author is using a common 17th-century Catholic argument: if the Protestant "Truth" was the original one, it could not have disappeared for over a millennium only to reappear in the 1500s.</note> it would have waited too long to appear], I shall never be like those wretched *Traitors and Apostates* until they themselves become the Apostolic and orthodox Church, which they never were.

5. That far from their so-called Reformation being that Church, one need only believe Mr. Pierre Gros <note>Pierre Gros was a former Protestant minister who converted to Catholicism in Turin in 1685, only to later return to Protestantism. The author is using Gros’s own signed abjuration against him.</note> to say that it is a collection of errors that have troubled the Church from Ebion and Cerinthus <note>Ebion and Cerinthus were early Christian heretics from the 1st and 2nd centuries; the author suggests the Reformation is just a revival of ancient, condemned ideas.</note> down to the latest Innovators <term>Innovators: A common derogatory term used by Catholic authors of this era to describe Protestants, implying they were creating "new" doctrines rather than following "ancient" tradition.</term>, as he declared in the first Article of his abjuration in Turin; the contrary of which he now dictates to his Copyists can only be to his own shame.

But because all these counter-accusations might upset these Gentlemen, I will content myself with offering for them the prayer that Our Lord made for those who pierced His Soul with outrages, as they did His Body with nails: <note>original: "Pere, pardonne leur, car ils ne savent ce qu'ils font."</note> *Father, forgive them, for they know not what they do.*

<margin>Luke 23:34.</margin>

If the truth were on their side, it is certain that by leaving their party I would have deserted it, which was never the case; and if the Catholic Religion were so abominable that by going from theirs to it, I had, as they say, fallen into *Atheism* <note>In the 17th century, the charge of "Atheism" was often used broadly to mean any belief system that led to a lack of proper fear of God or rejection of established moral order, not just the modern definition of a lack of belief in a deity.</note> by believing in neither God, nor Devil, nor Paradise, nor Hell, nor good, nor evil after this life, I consent that I would be...

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of his conversion. Having previously argued that the Reformers are the ones who abandoned the ancient Church, he now asserts that his return to Catholicism was a moral necessity to avoid "Atheism"—defined here as acting against one's own conscience and the known truth of God's Word.</meta>

...as criminal as they make me out to be for having joined its Communion?

But since I have recognized that the Catholic Church is the only true and salvific one in the world, that it believes in one God and one Savior, adores His Majesty in spirit and truth, and places all its trust in Him, listens to His will, and observes His orders: Thus, far from falling into Atheism for having embraced its Faith and joined its bosom; on the contrary, I would have been an Atheist if, knowing the truth, I had preached against it, as they seem so angry that I refused to do. And if, distrusting the truth of the promises of Jesus Christ that <margin>Matthew 16</margin> *the gates of Hell*, error, and heresy, *shall never prevail against His Church;* <margin>John 14:16</margin> and that by the guidance of His *Holy Spirit in all truth for eternity*, what it binds on earth, through its <margin>Matthew 16</margin> excommunications and anathemas, *shall be bound in Heaven*, I had wished—so as not to displease these Gentlemen—to wage war against God, His Word, and His <margin>Psalm 2:8</margin> Heritage, and thus perish myself, and cause my dear Homeland to perish under those excommunications and anathemas, despite my knowledge and conscience. 

Instead, I show [my homeland], by the Word of God and by all that has ever been most holy, most orthodox, and most venerable in the Church, that she is in <margin>2 Kings 6:20</margin> *Samaria* while she thinks she is in *Dothan* <note>The author is referencing a Biblical story where the prophet Elisha struck an army with blindness and led them into the heart of their enemy's capital, Samaria, while they believed they were still in Dothan. He is suggesting the Protestants are spiritually blind and being led to their ruin by their leaders.</note>. If she wishes to profit from the warnings I give her, let her withdraw from there and receive the consolation of such a salvific retreat.

And if she chooses to continue following the advice that will be no more fortunate for them in the future than it has been in the past, I am not guilty for not having done what lay in my power for her good and her salvation, and I will not be responsible for her when we all appear before the

--- Page 306 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s direct appeal to his former community. Having argued that his conversion to Catholicism was a matter of conscience rather than a betrayal, he now addresses the "Gentlemen of Salean" directly, explaining his motives for writing and apologizing for any harshness in his tone, which he attributes to the hostility of his opponents.</meta>

->### Tribunal of the just Judge of all men.<-

It is you, Gentlemen of Salean <note>Salean likely refers to the inhabitants of Salins or a similar regional community where the author formerly served or was born.</note>, who are that dear homeland for whose salvation I labor. God is my witness that I do so for no other motive than the affection I bear for you, not only through the bond of being born in the same place as you, but also because of the service I rendered to you over several years.

I could have spared myself all the trouble I am taking to make the truth known to you, and I could have worked toward my own salvation as I believed I ought to, leaving you the trouble of working toward yours as you pleased. However, I desire to reach out my hand to you and pull you from the labyrinth in which you find yourselves, though you do not see it.

To you is addressed the summary I sent to you in writing, and which I now send to you in print so that whoever wishes may benefit from it. For you, the book you may have seen was written, and to you once again this reply is addressed.

<margin>
Psalm 95.
Hebrews 3.
</margin>
The language I use here is drawn from the word of God, and the testimony I bring to confirm what I say to you comes from witnesses whom your Reformation <note>The author uses "votre Réforme" to refer to the Protestant Reformed Church from which he converted.</note> calls *Saints, most good, and most faithful*. God calls you to His salvation through the truth He manifests to you by my means; *do not harden your hearts lest, continuing always to err within them and not knowing His ways, He swears in His wrath that you shall never enter into His rest.* <note>Original: "n'endurcissés pas vos cœurs de peur que continuans toûiours à errer en eux, & à ne conêtre pas ses voies, il ne iure en son ire que vous n'entrerés iamais dans son repos." This is a paraphrase of Psalm 95:8-11 and Hebrews 3:7-11.</note>

If in this entire Treatise there are some terms less gentle than I would wish, you must attribute it to the rudeness of those <insert>terms</insert> which your Ministers <note>Referring to the Protestant clergy.</note> have used against the Catholic Religion and against me, rather than to any lack of goodwill I have for you or for them. For you have been able to judge for yourselves that the anger which

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of his former Protestant colleagues' response to his conversion. He accuses them of losing their temper and using deceitful tactics to prevent their congregation from considering Catholic truths. what they have shown in their writing has exceeded the limits of a just moderation, and shows too much passion to not make you suspicious of everything they have used to oppose the truths that I have laid out for you above.

<margin>Hebrews 10:39</margin>
They have filled their pamphlets <note>original: "Caïers," referring to the written booklets or quires used in theological disputes.</note> with insults against the Catholic Religion to make you wrongly abhor it forever; they have, as is their custom, attributed false things to it, they have falsified the texts of the Bible, truncated the testimonies of the Holy Doctors <note>The "Doctors of the Church" are influential early Christian theologians whose writings are considered authoritative.</note>, twisted my propositions into contradictions, disguised my proofs, and all their strength has consisted only of showing the weakness of their excuses to keep you, along with them, withdrawn from the Catholic Church of all the Ages of Christianity. May God grant them eyes to see, and a heart to better understand the true way of their salvation and of yours.

Meanwhile, Gentlemen, think of yourselves; you have the Scriptures, you will see what I am citing to you from them; compare them, and consider all the things contained therein without that common prejudice they inspire in you against the true Religion, which is that of your Sovereign <note>The author likely refers to King Louis XIV, emphasizing the link between the "true religion" and the state.</note>, of the State in which you live, and of all that is most sacred, most venerable, and most august under Heaven.

<margin>Galatians 6:5; Romans 14:12; Titus 3:10</margin>
It is a matter of salvation, Gentlemen; there is only one eternity to wait for after this life, either of happiness in Paradise, or of misery in hell; then, one shall not bear the burden of another: but each shall bear *his own*, and shall give an account of himself to God. Therefore, do not despise anything that is proposed to you for your own good; test everything, reject what is evil, and hold fast to what is good. If you take in good part all the salutary advice that I give you, you will do well by doing good. This is the third— <note>The text cuts off here at the word "troisième" (third), likely introducing a third point or section.</note>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's appeal. It follows the previous page's unfinished thought about "all that is most sacred... under Heaven," finishing the word "troisième" (third) as indicated by the catchword "fiéme." The author is wrapping up his argument that the Protestant Reformation is a modern "novelty" compared to the ancient Catholic Church.</meta>

third [exhortation] that I address to you; if you profit from it, it will be your consolation.

If you do otherwise, and if you always wish to follow all the contrary discourses in order <unclear>to</unclear> make you believe in the conformity of your Reformation to the word of God—as if our Lord had left his Church for 1,535 years without it <note>The author counts 1,535 years from the time of Christ to the mid-1530s, the era when John Calvin became prominent in Geneva.</note>, and as if they could instill in you that there is nothing more Divine and Apostolic than what they tell you against the Catholic Church—your Ministers <note>original: "Messieurs," a polite but firm way of referring to the Protestant pastors.</note> speak to you as if they themselves were the Apostles and Disciples of Jesus Christ, and their party the unique Sacred Bride of this adorable Savior. They preach that whoever separates from it is like those who separated themselves from the Apostolic Church to divide its union and create a separate Sect, or to fall back into Judaism and Paganism; while [their party] is but a novelty of a few years, born from the thoughts of men who wished to know more than everyone else from the fifteen previous centuries. I shall not extend my exhortations regarding your duty any further, having done so sufficiently in my Italian book to which I refer you again.

I finish with my prayers to this great JESUS, Savior of our souls, that it may please him to always enlighten you with his Divine lights to make you prefer his truth to the imaginations of the Innovators <note>original: "Nouateurs," a common 17th-century term for those who introduced new religious doctrines.</note>, and, by making you children of this Catholic or Universal Church that the Apostles command us to believe in,
<margin>Colossians 1:23</margin>
we may all one day receive with him the inheritance of the Saints in his light. Amen.
